• In NCHRH Bill, nothing is mentioned about inclusion and enrolment of Dental Hygienists.
• The Bill stipulates that the Commission, the Board and the Committee will be bound by
the directions of the Central Government. It may impinge on the objective functioning of
these bodies.
• The Bill lays down that the Commission, the Board and the Committee will have appointed
and nominated members and removal of Chairperson or any other member gives autocratic
powers to the Central Government.
• There is no provision for representation from professional organisations and health
universities or councils.
• Decisions of the Commission cannot be questioned in any court of law, which is against
the principles of natural justice.
• Only power of registration is restored with Councils; State Councils are made branches
of Central Council.
• The registration in State Council valid only for the State. This will only undermine State
Councils

Views/Comments of the Ministry
100. The Comments of the Ministry of Health and Family is given below point wise:–
• It is neither desirable nor possible to include each and every allied health science stream
as an independent profession in the Bill. Nonetheless, the Bill seeks to promote availability
of health care providers’ standardised quality and bridge the uneven distribution of the
existing work force. The Bill also has the provision to allow the Government to notify
constitution of National Councils for disciplines of health other than those mentioned in
the Bill. Moreover, the primary aim of NBHE would be to ensure augmentation of trained
specialists and super specialist in every field of health care.
• The Bill provides that the Commission will be guided by the directions given by the
Central Government only on the questions of policy. As regards provision to supersede

21
the Commission, Board, Committee and the National Councils, the same have been
incorporated for being exercised directly by the ‘Government in extraordinary circumstances
with a view to improve the overall functioning of the Commission with more transparency
and accountability.
• The Commission, Board and the Committee will not comprise of any nominated members.
They will have only appointed members duly selected by laid down procedure. The
Commission will also have the Presidents of the Council as members, who would come
through election. As stated above, the provision to remove or suspend Chairperson/
members has been kept for use in extraordinary circumstances.
• One of the main functions of the National Board for Health Education would be to
coordinate between medical and other scientific academies, societies, associations, institutions
and Government medical and scientific departments and services. This ensures participation
of professional bodies and health universities in the functioning of the Commission.
Furthermore, it is stated that sub-clause(4) of clause 3 stipulates that the Presidents of
each of the National Council shall be an ex-officio Member of the Commission and sub
clause (5) of clause 3 stipulates that all ex-office Members shall have voting rights.
• In so far as provisions under clause 100 are concerned it is stated that a commission
or tribunal is established with limited powers to decide and investigate matters falling
under its ambit so that ordinary courts are not overburdened. However such commissions/
tribunals are amenable to constitutional courts. Therefore a person aggrieved with the
decision of the Commission can always file an appeal in the High Court or the Supreme
Court and his fundamental rights would remain intact. The bill provides for an appellate
authority in the Central Government.
• The current regulatory bodies regulate both education and profession practice within their
domain and are over-burdened with all regulatory functions. NCHRH in fact seeks to
decentralise and trifurcate the regulatory functions among three separate bodies viz.
NBHE, NEAC and National Councils. The State Councils would be constituted by the
States under their own statute.
• The bill seeks to introduce the concept of live register for medical professional and
therefore it is essential to segregate the data. A person registered with the State Council
is not barred from practicing anywhere in India; he just needs to register himself with
the National Register to allow him to practice in any place outside his State that his
whereabouts and manpower planning is made easy.
• The Commission and its constituent bodies will be guided by eminent persons in health
profession with fresh and up to date outlook. They would be persons of standing in the
respective professions with integrity, administrative capability and outstanding ability.
• The Selection Committee would be constituted only for a prescribed period.
• The Bill only specifies that the CEO should not be a person below the rank of the
additional Secretary in Government of India. It does not bar a medical person from being
appointed as the CEO. It is a very crucial position and therefore the appointment should
be in the hands of the Government.
•  All the National Councils will be reconstituted as per the provisions of the Bill.

Views/Comments of the Ministry
102. The Comments of Ministry of Health and Family Welfare are given below pointwise:–
• The proposal to set up NCHRH has been extensively discussed with all stakeholders and
only after taking everyone on board the Government has gone ahead with the proposal.
The aim of the Bill is to create an overarching regulatory body for the health sector
regulating the various professions related to health under one umbrella. Different regulatory
bodies each responsible for its own cadre of workers have failed to provide a synergetic
approach to address the human resources needs in the health sector of the country. The
Committee itself would not be undertaking evaluation and assessment of institutes. It will
only lay down the standards, norms and processes for registration of evaluation and
assessment agencies with the Commission.
• The Bill seeks to introduce the concept of live register for all the health professionals,
to be maintained by respective State Councils. The National Councils and the State
Council shall maintain the register by such data capturing method as may be prescribed
by the Central or State Government and shall inter alia  contain the biometric and other
detail of each health professional which shall be verified at the time of renewal.
• The traditional system of medicine and the modern system of medicine are two different
approaches and bringing them together under a single regulatory body may result in
“AYUSH” getting relegated as an ancillary stream in the overall health delivery system.
• The NCHRH is proposed to be independent regulatory body and its constituent bodies
would comprise of eminent professionals from various field of health sciences as well as
law, management and technology. The Central Government, other than giving directions
on policy issues would have no say in the functioning of the Commission.
• In so far as provisions under clause 100 are concerned it is stated that a commission
or tribunal is established with limited powers to decide and investigate matters falling
under its ambit so that ordinary courts are not overburdened. However such commissions/
tribunals are amenable to constitutional courts. Therefore a person aggrieved with the
decision of the Commission can always file an appeal in the High Court or the Supreme
Court and his fundamental rights would remain intact. The bill provides for an appellate
authority in the Central Government.

23
• The present provisions do not bar a student from leaving the country after obtaining a
medical degree from a Government medical college. Under NCHRH such a student, after
acquiring higher qualification from abroad, would have to return to the country and serve
for three years failing which his registration shall be cancelled.
• The fact that there needs to be more clarity regarding the jurisdiction of the National
Councils and the State Councils has been noted and the clause shall be rectified in
consultation with the Legislative Department.
• The NCHRH Bill leaves a provision for the Government to notify constitution of National
Councils for any discipline of health other than those mentioned in the Bill.
Suggestions of Shri Kirti Azad, M.P. (L.S.)
103. The suggestions of Shri Kirti Azad, MP (L.S) are delineated below:–
• Not just that there is an acute shortage of hospital and their unequal distribution in various
States, the problem is also accentuated by the poor infrastructure and the poor quality of
education that is being given in most colleges. Dental and Medical institutions are
mushrooming all over, yet the quality of medical and paramedical education needs to be
strictly monitored. The concept of an overarching authority, is therefore, welcome.
• Nursing requires greater emphasis than ever before. Not only do we need to impart better
nursing education, we need to produce much larger number of nurses to improve the
Doctor-Nurses ratio. Instead of just expecting the private sector to churn out nurses and
pharmacists, the Government should establish institutes, alongside Government hospitals,
to train quality professionals. ESI, Army and CGHS hospitals can be conveniently used
for harnessing their capacity for training a larger pool of nursing professionals.
• Health is a State subject. This proposed legislation attempts to trample upon the role of
the States in as much as the proposed Commission does not give any representation to
the States. Whether it is the Central or the State Government, it should be mandated that
the members will only be from the Medical profession- there should be self control, and
at least two third members should be from Institutes of National importance, like AIIMS,
JIPMER etc.
• The process of removal of such eminent members should be tough. Unlike the Government’s
pushing out an eminent AIIMS Director Prof Venugopal purely for ego related reasons,
we need to incorporate the procedure for removing the members, which should only be
for very grave reasons. There should be assurance of tenure, and functional autonomy
and authority should be clearly defined and outlined in the proposed bill.
• There should be only one Selection Committee for all the three institutions, both in the
States as well as, in the Central Government, and there should be a minimum qualification,
experience, submission of technical papers in national/ international journals of repute
which should help zero in on the suitable candidates. To maintain quality standards,
doctors in private practice should not be more than 20% of the Committee’s strength.
• There should be no automatic approval of application for establishing educational institutions,
just by the efflux of one year. Every proposed institution should be inspected, preferably
by a panel of eminent doctors in that field to be constituted in every State, and on the
basis of the panel’s recommendation, an appropriate decision should be taken.
• Medical Research should be given prominence in the Bill. Even an Institution like AIIMS
is lagging behind, since it is unable to cope with the massive footfall every day. There
should be an incentive for research placed in the Bill, on the lines of Developed countries.

24
• Specialized institutions need to be encouraged to act as Teaching institutions for producing
quality faculty, contributing to research and at best as, tertiary level referral centre for
patients suffering from complicated disorders that cannot be managed elsewhere. With the
current load on even Institutions of National importance, they have become the first stop
for walk-in-patients, and that has impinged on the basic purpose for which they were
founded.
• Distance Education System in Medical Institutions should be a strict no-no. Like in
Management/ Law etc. Distance Education should not be considered at all. The Medical
profession deals with human life and having ‘professionals’ who are trained through
‘distance training methods’ can spell the end of the road for patient care.
• A new Institution should be allowed to come up after any State issues an Essentiality
Certificate, which should be done after making an objective assessment of manpower
requirements, to ensure better geographical spread and to meet the local needs.
104. The Committee took further oral evidence of Secretary and other officials, Department of
Health and Family Welfare on the 17
th August, 2012 to seek clarifications in the light of suggestions/
comments received from various stakeholders/experts. During his deposition, the Secretary stated that
his Ministry had received the copies of memoranda from the Rajya Sabha Secretariat containing views
and suggestions by different organizations and individuals and the comments thereon had been
furnished. The Secretary highlighted the fact that the dominant view that emerged from the memoranda
furnished to the Committee and evidences tendered before it was the pressing need for reform in the
health education sector. As regards the suggestions to bring AYUSH under the ambit of the Bill, he
emphasized the fact that AYUSH should be kept outside the ambit of the Bill as bringing it under a
single regulatory body may result in it being relegated to an ancillary stream in the overall health care
delivery system. Apprising the Committee of the consultative process undertaken prior to drafting of
the Bill, the Secretary stated that when the formation of the overarching body was envisaged, a task
force was constituted which prepared a report suggesting a model for National Commission for Human
Resources for Health (NCHRH). The report was sent to the State Governments and other stakeholders
and the draft NCHRH Bill was put on the website of the Ministry inviting comments and suggestions
thereon. He also stated that six regional consultations had also been held in which State Governments,
Vice-Chancellors, Principals of Medical Colleges, medical practitioners, academicians and representatives
of regional IMA had participated. He informed the Committee that the Government was open to
increasing the representations of State Governments in the National Commission, Board and Committee.
He tried to impress upon the Committee that the Bill would not usurp any powers that currently vest
with the State Governments. The registration with the State Councils and the National Councils, bar
of jurisdiction, provision for appellate authority, status of the courses run by National Board of
Examinations, formation of additional National Councils, etc. also figured in the discussion.
105. As regards the composition of the National Commission for Human Resources for Health, the
National Board for Health Education and the National Evaluation and Assessment Committee, the
Secretary submitted that those would be guided by eminent persons not only from disciplines of health
education but also from other disciplines like management, law etc. and the appointment of the
Chairpersons and Members would be based on the recommendation of a Selection Committee which
shall consist of persons of eminence and experience from medical and allied fields to avoid bias. As
regards Clause 100 which debars a person from calling into question any order under this Act, the
Secretary submitted that a Commission or Tribunal is established with limited powers to decide and
investigate matters falling under its ambit so that ordinary courts are not overburdened. However, all
Commissions are amenable to constitutional courts i.e., the High Courts and the Supreme Court. He
further stated that Clause 98 provides for an appellate authority. On the issue of separate councils for
allied health professionals like physiotherapists, occupational therapists, radiographers, medical technologists
etc., he stated that the Bill contains a provision for setting up of additional Councils.

25
106. The Secretary further informed that as per the Bill, a person enrolled in the State Council can
practice only in that State whereas a person registered with the National Council can practice
anywhere. He clarified that this was necessary because under the current system there was a lot of
confusion and there were cases where doctors have multiple registrations. He stated that actually a
person registered with the State Council is not barred from practicing anywhere in India but needs
to register himself with the National Register so that there is no duplication of figures involved.
Further, he stated that the Bill seeks to introduce the concept of live register for medical professionals
and, therefore, it is essential to segregate the data.

26
OBSERVATIONS/RECOMMENDATIONS OF THE COMMITTEE — AT A GLANCE
1. The Committee takes note of the fact that even though it is more than sixty years
since India attained independence, affordable healthcare and health education have been a
distant dream for the common people of the country. Even though concerted efforts have been
made by the Government, but due to substantial socio-economic and geographical inequalities,
those efforts have not made the desired impact. The Committee expresses its concern over the
acute shortage of qualified health workers including doctors in the country. It is constraining
to note that as per 2001 Census, the estimated density of all the health workers (qualified and
unqualified) in India is about 20 per cent less than the WHO norm of 2.5 workers (doctors,
nurses and midwives) per thousand population. This shows the substantial shortage of qualified
health workers in the country. The Committee also notes the disparities between the rural and
urban areas in respect of the availability of health infrastructure. Even though there is a steep
increase in the number of medical colleges in the country, the cause of the concern for the
Committee is that a number of colleges that have been opened are not evenly distributed. This
has resulted in distorted distribution of the country’s production capacity of health workers.
The Committee also takes cognizance of the fact that the other health professions such as
nursing, pharmacy, etc., are not in a promising state. The nursing education is also in a poor
condition resulting in poor quality of the nursing professionals. Similarly, the nurses-doctors
ratio in the country is only 0.8:1 as against the ideal ratio of 3:1. Adding to these woes is the
criticism being made against some of the National Health Councils, leading to judicial censure
on several occasions. The Committee, therefore, takes note of this background in which the
Bill has been brought forward by the Government in the Parliament.
2. Taking note of the importance of the Bill and its likely impact on the availability of
health professionals, health infrastructure and ultimately healthcare delivery for the common
people of the country, the Committee took the views of a cross-section of the society and
various stakeholders. The Committee feels that the need for reforms in health sector is long
overdue so as to invigorate the health sector. But several stakeholders have raised serious
apprehensions on various provisions of the Bill and effectiveness of various bodies that are
proposed to be established under the Bill. In view of the apprehensions expressed by various
stakeholders, the Committee, in its meeting held on 17 August, 2012, felt that the Bill, in the
present form, cannot be recommended. The Committee, therefore, decided not to go in for
clause-by-clause consideration of the Bill and to recommend to the Government to consider all
shades of opinion and all the suggestions and bring forward a revised comprehensive Bill
before the Parliament.
3. The Committee, however, makes the following general observations/recommendations to
enable the Government to take necessary action at the time of revisiting the Bill:
(i) The National Commission for Human Resources for Health, as proposed in the Bill, is
mandated to take measures to determine, maintain and coordinate the minimum
standards of and promote the human resources, in the disciplines of health education
and training, commensurate with the requirement of such resources in different States
and Union Territories. The Committee is aware that ‘Health’ is a State subject whereas
‘Health Education’ figures in the Concurrent List of the Constitution. However, the
composition of the Commission gives no representation to the States. The Committee
agrees with the viewpoint put forth by the State representatives that the States play a
26

27
vital role in delivery of healthcare and medical education. States are well versed with
existing medical education capacity and know their future requirements better. The
Secretary, Department of Health and Family Welfare, during the course of his deposition
before the Committee stated that he was open to giving greater representation to the
States in the National Commission, the National Board and the National Evaluation and
Assessment Committee. It is, in this context, that the Committee is of the considered
view that a substantive role should be mandated for the States in the Commission. The
Committee, therefore, recommends to revisit the institutions of National Commission,
National Board and National Evaluation and Assessment Committee and give adequate
representation to the States. Cooperation and coordination of the States is very essential
for better provision of healthcare and health education in the country. Discussions may
be held with all the State Governments before revising the Bill. Necessary modifications
may, accordingly, be made in the Bill.
(ii) Some stakeholders favoured strengthening of the existing Councils rather than
overarching body as proposed in the Bill. They felt that sufficient safeguards should be
provided in the present Councils to ensure their transparent functioning and accountability
to the Central Government and the Parliament. The Committee also took note of their
concern that the present National Councils have been relegated to maintaining the
Central Register only, in the Bill. There was also a mention that in the National
Commission, National Board and National Evaluation and Assessment Committee, the
representation of several professions has not been indicated. The Committee notes the
concern expressed by the Councils that their autonomy and democratic set-up have
been taken over under the Bill. The Committee feels that these apprehensions need to
be appropriately addressed by the Government in the Bill. There is a need for clarifying
all these concerns. The democratic functioning of the National Councils should be
appropriately protected, even if they are brought under the overarching body. As
regards the existing functions of the Councils, the Committee suggests that Councils
may be given the powers to consider all the proposals as per the existing functions and
after their due consideration, the three bodies proposed under the Bill i.e.  the
Commission, the Board and the Assessment Committee may be given the power to take
final decision in the respective matters. Besides, adequate representation should be
given to all the professions in the proposed Commission, Board and Committee.
(iii) Some of the stakeholders expressed their apprehensions that there is no element of
election in the composition of the Commission, Board and the Assessment Committee.
The Bill provides only for the appointment by the Central Government on the
recommendations of the Selection Committees. In fact, this has been objected by the
State Governments also. The Committee desires, that the apprehensions of stakeholders
may be considered by the Government while revising the Bill.
(iv) The Selection Committees proposed to be set up for recommending persons for
nominations to the Commission, Board and the Assessment Committee have been
questioned by some of the stakeholders. They felt that the selection process for the
Selection Committees has been made very ambiguous stating that the Chairperson and
Members shall be appointed in such manner as may be prescribed. The Committee
agrees that this would lead to doubts in the minds of the people and this needs to be
clearly spelt out. The Committee, in this regard, takes note of the Higher Education
and Research Bill, 2011 in which composition of the Selection Committee has been
clearly spelt out. The Committee recommends that a procedure on the similar lines be
spelt out at the time of revising the Bill. The Committee also feels that the members
of Selection Committee should be persons of eminence, preferably from the medical

28
field. Besides, the Committee also recommends that there should be only one Selection
Committee for all the three bodies.
(v) The Bill provides that the Chairperson or a Member of the National Commission/
National Board/National Evaluation and Assessment Committee can be removed by the
Central Government at its pleasure which is very ambiguous provision and susceptible
to misuse whereas the Higher Education and Research Bill, 2011 provides that the
Chairperson or a Member of the National Commission for Higher Education and
Research can be removed by the President. The Committee feels that a similar
provision may be incorporated in the present Bill. The Committee recommends that
adequate safeguards may be provided in the Bill so that the Chairperson and other
Members of the Commission, Board, and the Assessment Committee are able to
discharge their duties and responsibilities in a fair and objective manner.
(vi) It has been brought to the notice of the Committee that though the Bill seeks to
abolish the National Board of Examinations (NBE), it fails to define how the existing
streams of health education run by the NBE are to be preserved and promoted within
the ambit of the Bill. The Committee is given to understand that the NBE has provided
standardized examination for post-graduate courses across the country and public sector
hospitals like Railway Hospitals, Armed Forces Hospitals and some private sector
hospitals like Sir Ganga Ram Hospital, Shankar Netralaya, etc. are participating for the
post graduation courses. It has been impressed upon the Committee that India is very
short of specialists and the NBE provides an opportunity beyond the medical colleges to
train the specialists of higher order. The Committee agrees that the NBE performs very
important functions and the post-graduate medical education of the highest order is
being standardized by it, and if this stream disappears, it is going to affect the
specialists, who have been awarded degrees so far. The Committee, therefore,
recommends that the above apprehensions be adequately addressed and precise and
explicit provisions be made while revising the Bill to protect the existing streams of PG
education run by the NBE.
(vii) The Committee also takes note of the apprehensions expressed before it about a
potential conflict of powers between the Commission, the Board and the Assessment
Committee due to lack of clarity regarding the powers of the three bodies. One of the
apprehensions was that the Commission gives permission for new courses under Clause
17 of the Bill whereas Clause 30 gives an impression that the Board is fully empowered
to recognize new courses and give accreditation to new courses. Similarly, it was also
apprehended that there is conflict between Board and Committee regarding accreditation
of Health Educational Institutes under Clauses 30 (2) (t) and 37 (1) respectively. The
Committee strongly feels that there is a need to clearly demarcate the respective
jurisdictions of the three bodies under the Bill.
(viii) The Committee also takes note of the apprehensions expressed by some of the professional
associations like physiotherapy, dental hygienists, optometrists, occupational therapists
etc. They expressed the desire to have separate Council for each of the professions. For
example, Dental Hygienists Association felt that they are always relegated to the
background and they do not get sufficient prominence. They also felt that their
profession has not been appropriately represented in the Bill. The Committee feels that
many new fields have emerged in the health profession but the new fields are yet to
be granted the status of separate Council so as to ensure their better growth, regulation
and standards. The Committee, therefore, recommends that their grievances may also
be taken care of and separate Councils may be provided for them, wherever feasible.

29
(ix) The Committee takes note of the provision in Clause 17 (6) which provides that where
no order on establishment of institution for imparting health education or a new course
of study has been given by the Commission for a period of one year, the same shall
be deemed to have been approved by the Commission in the form in which it has been
submitted. The Committee expresses its serious doubts on this open-ended clause. The
Committee feels that this clause is susceptible to misuse by allowing backdoor entry of
health institutions or a new course of study by stalling the decision for one year, which
would automatically be treated as approval. The Committee recommends that this
provision may be made more stringent and sufficient riders and safeguards may be
provided in the clause.
(x) The Committee is also of the view that there is no mention about the Medical Research
in the preamble, powers and functions of the Commission nor has it been defined under
the definition in Clause 2. It has only been mentioned in Clause 30(1)(a) under the
powers and functions of the National Board of Examination stating that it is one of the
functions of the Board to maintain standards of Health Education and Research. Health
Research is covered under the Higher Education and Research Bill, 2011 also. The
Committee has noted that in the Higher Education Bill, 2011, Agricultural Education
and Research has been kept out of its purview. A comparative perusal of contents of
the provisions pertaining to jurisdiction and functions of the Commissions proposed
under both the Bills reveals that both the Bills have identical jurisdiction and functions
on various aspects of Medical Education and Research. Under such circumstances
overlap and conflict of jurisdiction is inevitable. Wherever there is overlap and conflict
of jurisdiction between more than one agency on a particular subject, the ultimate
sufferer would be its objective i.e. development of medical education and medical
research. The Committee is not in agreement with the Ministry’s contention that
Health Research requires a forum like National Commission on Higher Education.
There is a separate department for Medical Research mandated with the responsibility
of development of various aspects of Medical Research and coordination between various
National and International Agencies engaged in Medical Research. In the given
circumstances, the Committee is of the opinion that it would not be appropriate to keep
Medical Education and Medical Research under the jurisdiction of more than one
Agency and Ministry. It would not serve any purpose and rather it would hamper its
development. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that both Medical Education
and Medical Research should be brought under the purview of the proposed National
Commission envisaged in the Bill. The Ministry may appropriately address this issue
while revising the Bill.
(xi) The Committee notes that the medical education and healthcare under AYUSH has not
been brought under the Bill. The Committee, therefore, heard the views of the
Secretaries of Departments of Health and Family Welfare and AYUSH. Both the
Departments of Health and Family Welfare and Department of AYUSH desired to keep
the Indian Systems of Medicine and Homoeopathy out of the ambit of the present Bill
on the ground that the Allopathy and the Indian Systems of Medicine and Homoeopathy
are completely different and the latter needs focussed attention for proper development.
It was, therefore, proposed to be kept on a separate footing due to the apprehensions
that if they were brought under one Commission, the focussed attention of the AYUSH
may be lost. It was also brought to the notice of the Committee that a separate
Department was created in 1995 for Indian Systems of Medicine and Homoeopathy to
give focussed attention and later it was named as Department of AYUSH in 2003. A
separate policy known as ‘National Policy on Indian Systems of Medicine and

30
Homoeopathy’ was also formulated in 2002. The Committee cannot understand the
rationale behind having two separate overarching bodies for two different systems of
medicine within the country. The Committee is of the view that there should be only
one overarching body and all the health/medical professions should be brought under
one single umbrella though with separate Councils. The Committee, therefore,
recommends that the Indian Systems of Medicine and Homoeopathy may also be
brought under the jurisdiction of the National Commission for Human Resources for
Health. The representatives of the Councils of the Indian Systems of Medicine and
Homoeopathy may also be given representation in all the bodies, i.e., the Commission,
the Board as well as the Assessment Committee so that their interests are well taken
care of.
(xii) The Committee notes that though Health Educational Institutions, Health Institutions
and Health Education have been mentioned in the Bill, but Health Education has not
been defined while Health Educational Institution or Health Institutes have been
defined. The Committee desires that this may be amply clarified. The Committee also
desires that health education should be replaced by medical education because it is not
the Health Educational Institution, it is Medical Educational Institution which imparts
various kinds of medical education. The Committee, therefore, recommends that Health
Education, Health Education Institutions/Health Insitution may be replaced by Medical
Education/Medical Educational Institutions/Medical Institutions whereever they appear
and Medical Education may be appropriately defined.
(xiii) Similarly, distance education system as has been mentioned in Clause 2(r) is also not
acceptable to the Committee. The Committee feels that Medical Education should not
be imparted through distance education mode and it should be a regular course.
4. The Committee has received several suggestions from various stakeholders in the form
of written representations, written submissions as well as oral evidence. The Committee has
dealt with some important suggestions made by various stakeholders and appended all the
memoranda/written submissions to the Report. The memoranda/written submissions received
from various persons/bodies had been sent to the Ministry for comments. The issues raised by
various persons/bodies in the memoranda and the written submissions and the comments of
the Ministry are appended. Some of the stakeholders have proposed amendments to various
provisions of the Bill. The Committee recommends that the Ministry of Health and Family
Welfare may carefully examine all the suggestions made by various stakeholders in the written
memoranda, written submissions and oral evidence and also the recommendations made by this
Committee while revising the Bill. All the apprehensions made by various stakeholders may
be appropriately addressed. If need be, the Ministry may hold another round of discussions
with all the stakeholders before finalizing the fresh Bill.
5. The Committee, accordingly, recommends that the Ministry may withdraw this Bill and
bring forward a fresh Bill after sufficiently addressing all the views, suggestions and the
concerns expressed. Before finalising the fresh Bill, the Ministry may hold discussions with all
the stakeholders including the State Governments.

2. In the absence of the Chairman of the Committee, Dr. Sanjay Jaiswal, member of the
Committee was elected to the Chair. At the outset, he welcomed Members of the Committee and
apprised them about the agenda of the meeting i.e. to hear the views of the Secretary, Department
of Health and Family Welfare on the National Commission for Human Resources for Health Bill, 2011.
3. The Committee then heard the Secretary and other officers of the Department of Health and
Family Welfare on the National Commission for Human Resources for Health Bill, 2011. The Secretary
gave a brief background on the Bill.
4. Thereafter, the Joint Secretary, Department of Health and Family Welfare made a power-point
presentation. The presentation included the composition of Commission, mode of appointment and
distinctive features i.e. power of Central Government to issue directions on issue of policy; power to
remove Chairman of Bodies constituted under it by the Central Government, provision for punishment
under clauses 75-85 which included imprisonment; mandate given to State Governments to form
councils within three years from the passing of the Act; renewal of registration after every ten years;
maintenance of live registers; provision for overseas citizens of India to practice in the country, etc.
5. During the course of the meeting, the members raised some queries including Administrative
Control of Ministry over the Commission; need for bringing such a bill; reasons of exclusion of
traditional system of medicine (AYUSH) from the ambit of Bill; need for bringing physiotherapists
under the separate council; details of medical and dental colleges in Government and private sectors;
details of countries which have such overarching regulator for health sector; rationale behind setting
up of separate State Councils; impact of the Bill on simplification of procedures. The Committee
sought to have details regarding number of seats in Government and private medical colleges; and
copy of Act governing General Medical Council of United Kingdom, composition of the drafting
Committee and Task Force alongwith details of the meetings of the Committee/ Task force and
verbatim records of the proceedings; etc. The Secretary and other officers of the Department of
Health and Family Welfare answered some of the queries. The Committee directed the Secretary,
Department of Health and Family Welfare to send written replies to queries which remained unanswered,
within a week’s time.
6. A Verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.

Dr. V . M. Katoch, Secretary
2. In the absence of the Chairman of the Committee, Dr. Sanjay Jaiswal, Member of the
Committee was elected to the Chair. At the outset, he welcomed Members of the Committee and
apprised them about the agenda of the meeting i.e. to hear the views of the Secretaries of the
Departments of Health and Family Welfare, AYUSH, Health Research, Ministry of Health and Family
Welfare and the Secretary, Department of Legal Affairs & Additional Secretary of the Legislative
Department, Ministry of Law and Justice on the issue of inclusion of various disciplines of AYUSH
under the ambit of the National Commission for Human Resources for Health (NCHRH) Bill, 2011 and
research related aspects in the Bill.
3. Thereafter, the Committee heard the Secretary, Department of AYUSH on the issues of
inclusion of various disciplines of AYUSH in the ambit of the Bill. The Secretary, during his deposition,
inter alia , made the following submissions: (i) There is need to regulate, protect and promote the
traditional Indian system of medicines. (ii) There has been tremendous progress in AYUSH since 1995
when the Department of AYUSH was set up as an independent Department. (iii) The requirements of
Indian systems of medicine in hospitals as well as in teaching colleges are different from the Allopathic
system of medicine and if both the systems of medicines i.e. modern and traditional are put under
a single Commission, Indian systems of medicines may lose focus as needed to nurture them. (iv) The
Department of AYUSH was created to give boost and prominence to Indian systems of Medicine and
Homoeopathy and take them out from the shadow of modern medicine and if it does not get separate
Commission, it will revert back to the pre-1995 position. The Secretary also solicited the Committee’s
support for a separate Commission for Indian Systems of Medicines and Homoeopathy. Thereafter, the
Members raised some queries which were answered by Secretary of the Department.
4. The Committee, then, heard the Secretary, Department of Health and Family Welfare on the
issue of inclusion of AYUSH under the NCHRH, Bill, 2011. The Health Secretary inter alia  informed
that the modern system of medicine and the traditional systems of medicine are two different
approaches and bringing them together under a single regulatory body may result in AYUSH getting
relegated as an ancillary stream in the overall health delivery system. He also informed that the
proceedings for drafting the Bill were initiated by a task force established under the Chairmanship of
Dr. M.K. Bhan. To finalize the implementation of the recommendations of the said task force, another
task force was set up which decided to exclude Indian traditional system of medicine from the ambit
of the NCHRH Bill.

37
5. Thereafter, the Secretary, Department of Health Research deposed before the Committee. In
reply to a query, he informed the Committee that the research was part of the said Bill and was
included in Clause 30 of the Bill which states that “ The Board shall take measures to facilitate and
promote academic studies and research in emerging areas of health education with focus on professional
health education and to ensure uniform augmentation of trained specialists and super specialists, to
achieve excellence in these and connected areas”. He, however, admitted that the word “research” did
not find a mention in the Preamble. The Members were of the opinion that research aspect should
be reflected in the ‘Preamble’ and directed the Department of Health and Family Welfare to make
necessary amendments in the said Bill. Members raised queries which were replied to by the
witnesses. The Committee then directed the Secretary, Department of Health and Family Welfare to
send written replies to queries which remained unanswered, within a week’s time.
6. A verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
7. The Committee adjourned at 4.01 
P.M.

2. At the outset, the Chairman welcomed Members of the Committee and apprised them about
the agenda of the meeting i.e. to hear the views of the representatives of Pharmacy Council of India,
Indian Nursing Council, Central Council of Homoeopathy and Central Council of Indian Medicine on
the National Commission for Human Resources for Health Bill, 2011.
3. The Committee then heard the views of Shri. D. Chakraborty, Vice-President, Pharmacy
Council of India, on the said Bill. He stated that pharmacy has both health orientation and industrial
orientation and the issues have not been addressed in NCHRH Bill, 2011 in its present form. He
highlighted issues like composition of pharmacy council in the said Bill, purpose behind separate State
and Central registration; composition of State Councils not finding any mention in the Bill, no
representation of pharmacy in the Board and the three Committees formed under ambit of NCHRH Bill;
conflict between existing assessment and examination committee in Pharmacy Council of India under
AICTE and Pharmacy Act 1948 and the committee as proposed in the NCHRH Bill etc. He suggested
that the term Medical Colleges/Institutes wherever exists in the Bill should be replaced by ‘Health
Institutions’ to also include pharmacy. He further stated that Diploma in Pharmacy should be a
recognized qualification. He further stated that PCI wants pharmacy to be integrated into mainstream
health care delivery system and is in favour of the Bill with some modifications.
4. Thereafter, Mr. T. Dileep Kumar, President, INC, apprised the Committee about the ideal
viz-a-viz present statistics of nurse to patient and physician ratio and nursing census project, Universal
health coverage, nurse midwifery practitioner program etc. He also suggested that there is need to
focus on strengthening the existing nursing organization structure.

42
5. The Committee then heard the views of Shri Vaidya Raghunandan Sharma, President, CCIM.
He stated that CCIM is not in favour of inclusion of Indian System of Medicine in NCHRH Bill, 2011.
He justified the non-inclusion of Indian Systems of Medicine in NCHRH Bill, stating that they are based
on different ideologies, concepts and practice; there are conflicts of views between allopathic and
homoeopathic doctors; there would be difficulty in formulation of policies of Indian system of
medicine on global level, etc. He also informed about the current initiatives taken to standardize the
education and practice of Indian Systems of Medicine. Thereafter, Dr. Ramjee Singh, President,
Central Council of Homoeopathy discussed the development in the field of Homoeopathy since
Council’s constitution and stated that homoeopathy has not developed much and a lot still needs to
be done to promote it so as to make it a part of NCHRH Bill, 2011. He also stated that there is need
to enhance the autonomy and budgetary allocations for Homoeopathy.
6. During the course of the meeting, the Members raised queries some of which were answered
by the witnesses. The Committee directed the witnesses to send written replies to queries which
remained unanswered, within a week’s time.
7. A Verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
8. The Committee adjourned at 5.00 
P.M.

2. At the outset, the Chairman welcomed Members of the Committee and apprised them about
the agenda of the meeting i.e. to hear the views of the representatives of the Board of Governors of
Medical Council of India and Dental Council of India on the National Commission for Human
Resources for Health Bill, 2011.
3. The Committee first heard Prof. K. K. Talwar, Chairman, Medical Council of India on the Bill.
While supporting the structure of the said Bill in general, he submitted that the Bill would result in
imbibing high sense of professionalism and interaction among all the councils which is lacking in
current scenario and would further clear out division of responsibilities. He, however, felt that there
is need to redefine the selection committee. He also stated that if regulations are followed and proper
implementation is done, the Bill would be able to achieve its intended objectives. The Members also
discussed other issues like state of health research, distortions in Medical Council of India, need for
more medical colleges, graduate and Post graduate seats in medical colleges, duration of rural
internship etc. Further, Dr. Talwar also stated that the quality of medical education has degraded since
last 2-3 decades.
4. Thereafter, Dr. Dibyandu Majumdar, President, Dental Council of India (DCI) stated that the
NCHRH Bill is unconstitutional and is against the policy of DCI. Clauses 100 and 101 are undemocratic
since there are no elections; Clause 115(2) is autocratic; there are less number of members in the
newly formed committees; roles of State councils have not been defined except to maintain registers
and attending seminars etc. He also informed the Committee that there are 290 dental colleges across
the country out of which 40 are Government colleges. He further apprised the Committee about the
new initiatives being undertaken or proposed by DCI including starting satellite centers by various
colleges to provide free dental services, three months mandatory rural internship for dental graduates,
provision of dental surgeon at primary health centers, increase in post graduate seats etc. He suggested
that the current process of getting approvals for dental colleges should be reversed, there should be
more authority to DCI, there is need for transparency in inspection process and to increase number
of staff in Government colleges. He also added that the existing councils should be strengthened
instead of abolishing them.
5. During the course of the meeting, the members raised some queries which were answered by
the witnesses. The Committee directed the witnesses to send written replies to queries which remained
unanswered, within a week’s time.
6. A Verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
7. The Committee adjourned at 1.30 
P.M.

47
X. Legislative Department
1. Dr. G . Narayana Raju, Joint Secretary and Legislative Counsel
2. Shri N. R. Battu, Additional Legislative Counsel
3. Shri R. Sreenivas, Deputy Legislative Counsel
XI. Department of Legal Affairs
Shri Satish Chandra, Joint Secretary and Legal Advisor
2. At the outset, the Chairman welcomed Members of the Committee and apprised them of the
agenda of the meeting, i.e. hearing views of the representatives of Indian Medical Association, Delhi
Medical Association, Indian Association of Physiotherapy (Central), and some other Physiotherapist
Forums/Associations on the National Commission for Human Resource for Health Bill, 2011. The
Chairman also informed Members that the Committee was earlier granted extension of time till
31
st May, 2012 and again till 15 th July, 2012, for presentation of its Report on the aforesaid Bill. The
Committee, however, felt that since a large number of stakeholders are yet to be heard and memoranda
received on the said Bill also need thorough examination which would entail considerable time, it would
not be possible for it to complete all stages of consideration of the Bill and present its Report before
15
th July, 2012. The Committee, therefore, decided to seek further extension of time till the last week
of the Monsoon Session of Parliament 2012, for presenting its Report on the Bill, and authorized its
Chairman to approach Hon’ble Chairman, Rajya Sabha for the purpose.
3.  The Chairman also informed the Members that the Committee had earlier sought the written
views of all State/UT Governments. However, only 6 State/UT Governments have so far responded.
He therefore suggested that the Committee should hear Chief Secretaries of all the remaining State/
UT Governments and also representatives of medical colleges/institutes of different States before
finalizing its views on the Bill. The Committee agreed to the suggestion and decided that it should hear
Chief Secretaries of all the remaining State/UT Governments including some medical institutions/
colleges/ medical universities functioning under State Governments.
4. Thereafter, the Committee first heard views of Dr. S. Arul Rhaj, Past National President, IMA
on NCHRH Bill, 2011. He presented his views by way of a power-point presentation. While strongly
opposing the said Bill, he inter alia  submitted that the current framework of the Bill would not allow
it to achieve its listed objectives and that the case for abolishing the existing institutions/councils is
unconvincing. He further stated that though the Bill had defined the word ‘Medical’ as ‘Modern
Medical’ and ‘Medical Practitioner’ as ‘Modern Medical Practitioner’; it has failed in defining Dentistry,
AYUSH, Nursing, Pharmacy, Pharmacist and Paramedical Personnel; etc. He also lamented the fact that
the Bill in its present form would seriously erode powers of the State Governments. He concluded by
stating that there was a need to retain the autonomous representative character of the existing medical
and allied councils which were functioning in a transparent manner.
5. The Committee then heard Dr. Harish Gupta, President, Delhi Medical Association on the said
Bill. During his deposition, while endorsing the views of IMA, he added that all members of three
bodies under NCHRH Bill, 2011 would be appointed members, thereby leading to loss of their
democratic character. He was, therefore, of the view that there is a need for more elected members
in the envisaged bodies to ensure that the democratic character of these bodies is kept intact. He also
stated that the prime objective of the said bill is to ensure better Human Resource Development and
better Health education but was of the opinion that this could not be achieved with the proposed
system. He was of the view that the autonomous character of the existing MCI should be restored.
6. The Committee also heard the views of certain Physiotherapy Associations on the Bill by way
of power point presentations. During their deposition, they inter alia  stated that the 31
st report of the

48
Committee had strongly recommended for formation of a separate Physiotherapy Council. They also
stated that even the Planning Commission Report 2002-2007 had recommended favorably in this
regard. They were, therefore, of the view that there is a need to include independent Physiotherapy
Council in the present Bill to ensure better regulation of Physiotherapy profession.
7. During the course of the meeting, the members raised queries, some of which were answered
by the witnesses. The Committee directed the witnesses to send written replies to queries which
remained unanswered, within a week’s time.
8. A Verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
9. The Committee then adjourned at 4.45 
P.M.

4. Shri Radhey Shigam, Member
5. Shri Yogender Sharma, Member

2.  At the outset, the Chairman welcomed Members of the Committee and apprised them of the
agenda of the meeting, i.e. hearing views of the representatives of  the National Board of Examinations;
Indian Association of Physiotherapists, Mumbai; All India Dental Hygienist Association, New Delhi;
Prof. Aseem Prakash Tikku, Faculty of Dental Sciences, Chhatrapati Shahuji Maharaj Medical University,
Lucknow; and Dr. Rohit Khanna, Lucknow on the National Commission for Human Resources for
Health (NCHRH) Bill, 2011.
3. Thereafter, the Committee first heard views of Prof. K. Srinath Reddy, President, National
Board of Examinations (NBE) on NCHRH Bill, 2011. He gave an overview of the pressing need for
redefinition of health education, health system connectivity, need for alignment of the health care
delivery system with the needs of the country, functioning of NBE since its inception, initiatives taken
by the NBE. While not opposing the vision of the present Bill he voiced concerns on the abolition of
the existing National Board of Examinations under the proposed Bill which would lead to a complete
watering down of the functions being carried out by the present NBE. He was of the view that there
is a need to protect and preserve the functions of NBE. He also pointed out a number of infirmities
in respect of certain Clauses of the proposed Bill viz. Clauses 16(1)(e),(f);17;19; 24;30(d),(e);37(1);
30(2)(t);24(3);24(4);28(1), etc. He emphasized the need to integrate different streams of medical
education but stated that the Bill does not have any provision in this regard. He concluded his
presentation by stating that although the fundamental autonomy of the individual councils has been
targeted, the ethical standards and the need for integration among various professions has not been
addressed to. Moreover, the proposed Bill is heavily tilted in favour of regulation by bureaucrats.
4.  The Committee then heard Dr. Ali Irani, President, Indian Association of Physiotherapists,
Mumbai on the said Bill. He inter-alia gave the history, growth and significance, of physiotherapy in

51
India. He also expressed the need for inclusion of a separate council physiotherapy in the said Bill as
had also been recommended in the 31 st Report of the Committee on the Physiotherapy Central Councils
Bill, 2007.
5. The Committee further heard the views of Aseem Prakash Tikku, Faculty of Dental Sciences,
Chhatrapati Shahuji Maharaj Medical University, Lucknow and Dr. Rohit Khanna, Lucknow on the Bill.
Prof Tikku, during his deposition before the Committee welcomed the Bill, stating that the Bill was
well-drafted. However, he advocated that some of the part-time members of the envisaged Commission
should be elected and the rest may be selected. With regard to the Dental Council of India, he
informed the Committee that the present council was headed by private practitioners for the last
twenty years which was against the Government policy. Dr. Rohit Khanna also was in favour of
nominated persons. He, however, was of the view that eminent persons of medical fraternity should
only be nominated to the Commission. Lastly, he concluded by submitting that State Councils must
have more teeth to curb malpractices and defined powers must be there to take action at that level.
6. The Committee finally heard the views of Shri S. K. Pandey, President, All India Dental
Hygienist Association, on the said Bill. He was of the view that Dental Hygiene may be given a
separate status in the proposed Bill. He also stressed on the need to upgrade the present Dental
Hygiene diploma by lateral entry to the Bachelor of Dental Sciences (BDS) as it would result in
horizontal and vertical growth and also open promotion avenues which are not present there under the
scheme of Dental Hygiene diploma.
7. During the course of the meeting, the members raised queries, some of which were answered
by the witnesses. The Committee directed the witnesses to send written replies to queries which
remained unanswered, within a week’s time.
8. A Verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
9. The Committee then adjourned at 4.45 
P.M. to meet again on 18 th July, 2012.

WITNESSES
1. Dr. H.N. Tripathi, Chief Medical Superintendent, Sahara Hospital, Lucknow
2. Prof. D. K. Gupta, Vice Chancellor, Chhatrapati Shahuji Maharaj Medical University,
Lucknow.
2. At the outset, the Chairman welcomed Members of the Committee and apprised them of the
agenda of the meeting, i.e. hearing views of the representatives of  Prof. D.K. Gupta, Vice Chancellor,
Chhatrapati Shahuji Maharaj Medical University, Lucknow, and  Dr. H.N. Tripathi, Chief Medical
Superintendent, Sahara Hospital, Lucknow, on the National Commission for Human Resource for
Health Bill, 2011.
3. Thereafter, the Committee first heard views of Dr. Tripathi, President, Chief Medical
Superintendent, Sahara Hospital, Lucknow on NCHRH Bill, 2011. He stated that academic and
intellectual independence should be protected. He was of the view that there was a need in the present
Bill to involve a more measured and balanced view towards the Medical Council of India (MCI) and
there was a need to include more medical and other eminent persons form the corporate and
Government Hospitals and if need be a Search Committee may be set up to help in the selection of
suitable persons to the top posts in MCI. He was of the view that the present Bill would discourage
youngsters to enter the profession as there was too much regulation under the Bill. Further, the present
MCI Act was fine, however a stringent auditing system was the need of the hour. He pointed out that
the Bill bars the aggrieved party from going to court which will give rise to unethical practices. He
further opined that the MCI body can be strengthened by including eminent medical professionals for
shorter terms; there is a need for strengthening the auditing and monitioring system; decentralization
of powers must be done; severe punishment must be meted out for malpractices; Advisory Committee
should be given powers to monitor the office bearers, etc.
4.  The Committee then heard Prof. D.K. Gupta, Vice Chancellor, Chhatrapati Shahuji Maharaj
Medical University, Lucknow on the Bill. He inter-alia submitted that the proposed overarching council
in the Bill should be governed by persons of eminence and impeccable integrity from the medical
fraternity and no bureaucrat should be there; selection process of the Chairman should be transparent;
the chairman and members of the envisaged Commission, members of National Board, National
evaluation and selection Committee and National/State Councils should be appointed by a search
selection committee, etc.
5. During the course of the meeting, the members raised queries, some of which were answered
by the witnesses. The Committee directed the witnesses to send written replies to queries which
remained unanswered, within a week’s time.
6. The Committee also directed the Secretariat to circulate for the perusal of Members a
document of Medical Council of India titled – Vision, 2015.
7. A Verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
8. The Committee then adjourned at 12.00 Noon .

1. Prof. R.C. Deka, Director, All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi
2. Prof. Y .K. Chawla, Director, Post Graduate Institute of Medical Education and Research,
Chandigarh
54

55
3. Dr. P.H. Ananthanarayanan, Senior Professor and Head, Department of Biochemistry,
Jawaharlal Institute of Post-Graduate Medical Education and Research, Puducherry
4. Dr. P. Satishchandra, Director-Vice-Chancellor, National Institute of Mental Health and
Neuro Sciences (NIMHANS), Bangalore
2. At the outset, the Chairman welcomed Members of the Committee and apprised them of the
agenda of the meeting, i.e. hearing views of  Prof. R.C. Deka, Director, All India Institute of Medical
Sciences, New Delhi; Prof. Y .K. Chawla, Director, Post Graduate Institute of Medical Education and
Research, Chandigarh; Dr. P.H. Ananthanarayanan, Senior Professor and Head (Department of
Biochemistry), Jawaharlal Institute of Post Graduate Medical Education and Research, Puducherry; and
Dr. P. Satishchandra, Director-Vice-Chancellor, National Institute of Mental Health and Neuro Sciences
(NIMHANS), Bangalore on the National Commission for Human Resource for Health (NCHRH)
Bill, 2011.
3. Thereafter, the Committee first heard views of Prof. R.C. Deka, Director, All India Institute
of Medical Sciences on NCHRH Bill, 2011. He submitted before the Committee that there was a need
to take a fresh look at the health needs of the country as the present system had created inequitable
distribution of human resources for health and medical institutions in the country. He dwelt into the
vision provided by the Bhore Committee in envisioning the health care needs of the country following
which by creation of an Act of the Parliament, Medical Council of India (MCI) came into being in
1956. He therefore was of the view that before passage of the proposed Bill there was a need for
serious introspection of the deficiencies in the present health care delivery system and creation of an
expert Committee on the lines of Bhore Committee to examine the need for healthcare professionals.
He delineated the lacunae existing in the structure of the proposed Bill viz. aims and objectives of the
Bill being not in line with the rapid advancements in the field of Medical sciences. He also stated that
there was need for new knowledge in medical field owing to new technical advancements in the
medical field all over the world, greater delegation of powers to the State Government, universities and
medical institutions, change in the nomenclature ‘Health Education’ to ‘Health Professional Education’,
participation of State Governments and Private Institutions/NGOs in framing of the Bill, creation of
civil services for Health to ensure equitable distribution of Health care personnel and quality assurances
thereof, inclusion of Health Research in the proposed Bill, comparative study of systems prevalent in
other countries. He further touched up on issues like lack of any mention of Institutions of National
importance in the Bill; creation of a medical practitioners’ tribunal to take care of malpractices in health
care; and owing to central quota in State Medical Institutions, need for granting there central
assistance etc.
4. The Committee then heard Prof. Y .K. Chawla, Director, Post Graduate Institute of Medical
Education & Research (PGIMER) on the Bill. He inter-alia submitted that even though the proposed
Bill was a good document, it was silent on status of Institutions of National importance like PGIMER.
He also highlighted the absence of defined procedure for regulation of private medical colleges under
the said Bill, need for pan India registration, absence of clarity in doctoral and post doctoral medical
qualifications in Schedule II of the Bill and doctoral and post doctoral dental qualification in Schedule
III of the Bill, need for inclusion of Members (primarily academic) from Medical Institutes (both
private and Government) in the Bill, need for inclusion of a clause in the Bill to include collaborations
by way of MoUs with international universities, need for inclusion of adjunct facility in the proposed
Bill, etc.
5.  The Committee then heard the views of Dr. P.H. Ananthanarayanan, Senior Professor and
Head (Department of Biochemistry), Jawaharlal Institute of Post Graduate Medical Education and
Research on the Bill. He inter alia  submitted his comments on various changes/alterations required in
various provisions of the Bill viz. in Chapter II clause 7- the chairperson and other members should
give declaration of their and family members’ immovable and movable properties at the time of

56
assuming charge and every year thereafter which would be displayed on the website of the Commission;
Chapter II clause 8(1)- duration for not accepting employment by Chairman and other Members on
ceasing to hold office may be increased from 2 to 5 years; Chapter II clause 10(2)-if the period of
absence of chairperson is less than 1 week, the acting chairperson shall not take any policy decision
or major financial decisions; Each Institution should get permission from the Commission in order to
ensure the quality of medical education. In case of delay in getting response from the Commission,
a mechanism or agency should be created /named to whom the affected Institution can approach;
Chapter III (National Board for Health Education) clause 30(2)- besides organising the above it should
also provide financial support for organising seminars,trainings,etc.; Chapter IV clause 37(4)- may be
changed from “same evaluation and assessment agency” to “ same evaluation and assessment agency
or persons”, Chapter IV- clause 40(1) may meet at least once in two months (considering their
responsibility) and the quorum should be at least three(The Total number of members in this
Committee is 7); Chapter V (National Councils and State Councils) clause 57(1)- reduction of
enrolment period from 10 years to 5 years.
6.  The Committee finally heard the views of Dr. P. Satishchandra, Director-Vice-Chancellor,
National Institute of Mental Health and Neuro Sciences (NIMHANS) on the Bill. He inter alia
submitted his comments on various changes/alterations required in various provisions of the Bill viz.
the Bill should be named National Commission for Human Resources for Health Sciences Bill, 2011
instead of the NCHRH Bill, 2011; in Chapter II, qualification for the members should be reduced to
10 years for Professor and additional professor; experience for being a member of the Selection
Committee be reduced to 15 years to encourage younger people; make provisions for promoting
exchange of visiting professors from Institutions of Higher Education and Research to Institutions of
Health Research; Chapter VIII, all regional medical centres should have a Department of Medical
Biotechnology in addition to all the other departments with facility for training; every regional medical
centre must have a separate Department of Disaster Management and Critical Care; need for inclusion
of innovative approaches to teaching in Health Sciences especially Medical Science, etc.
7. During the course of the meeting, the members raised queries, some of which were answered
by the witnesses. The Committee directed the witnesses to send written replies to queries which
remained unanswered, within a week’s time.
8. A Verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
9. The Committee then adjourned at 12.55 
P.M. to meet again on 31 st July, 2012 .

2. The Committee elected Shri Derek O’ Brien to the Chair due to slight delay in arrival of the
Chairman of the Committee to the meeting on account of some prior commitments. He informed the

and sought exemption from appearance for the meeting while the Principal Secretaries of Gujarat and

Chairman welcomed Members of the Committee and apprised them of the agenda of the meeting, i.e.
hearing views of Shri Sanjay Mitra, Principal Secretary, Government of West Bengal and Shri Rajesh
Kishore, Principal Secretary, Department of Health and Family Welefare, Gujarat on the National
Commission for Human Resource for Health (NCHRH) Bill, 2011. The Committee expressed its
displeasure and decided not to hear views of the Joint Director of the Maharashtra Government and
directed to the Secretariat to convey its displeasure to the State Government.
3. Thereafter, the Committee first heard views of Shri Sanjay Mitra, Principal Secretary, Government
of West Bengal on NCHRH Bill, 2011. He inter alia  highlighted before the Committee issues like the
present Bill promoting curtailment of self-regulation and regulation by bureaucrats, need for strengthening
the existing Councils instead of bringing an overarching Council, need for a role of State Governments,
in selection of Chairman and Members of the National Commission, Board and the Committee as
proposed in the Bill, selection of Chairman on the lines similar to the Chairman of the National Human
Rights Commission (NHRC), need to specify the status of the existing councils in the proposed Bill,
lengthy appeals procedure under the proposed Bill,, continuance of existing Acts/regulations, etc.
4.  The Committee then heard Shri Rajesh Kishore, Principal Secretary, Department of Health and
Family Welefare, Gujarat on the Bill. He highlighted issues like the need for reforms in medical
education and importance of setting standards in medical education; he apprehended that the proposed
bill was too lengthy and so overarching that it might lead to difficulties in coordination and consultative
mechanism between the National Commission, National Board for Health Education, National Evaluation
and Assessment Committee, the three bodies proposed in the Bill. He also highlighted that the bodies
proposed in the Bill are largely nominative in nature; absence of role of State Governments in the Bill
was a serious drawback as the concerned State being aware of its needs was the best judge to grant
permission for setting up medical institution in the concerned state; Medical education being in the
concurrent list there was a need to have representation of State Governments in the proposed
Commission and Board; the institutional framework proposed in the Bill would make things more
complicated etc.
5. During the course of the meeting, the members raised queries, some of which were answered
by the witnesses. The Committee directed the witnesses to send written replies to queries which
remained unanswered, within a week’s time.
6. A Verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
7. The Committee then adjourned at 12.15 
P.M.

59
XXI
TWENTY-FIRST MEETING
(2011-12)
The Committee met at 10.00 A.M. on Friday, the 17 th August, 2012 in Committee Room ‘A’,
Ground Floor, Parliament House Annexe, New Delhi.
MEMBERS PRESENT
1. Shri Brajesh Pathak — Chairman
RAJYA SABHA
2. Shri Arvind Kumar Singh
3. Dr. Prabhakar Kore
4. Shri Derek O’ Brien
LOK SABHA
5. Dr. Sucharu Ranjan Haldar
6. Dr. Monazir Hassan
7. Dr. Sanjay Jaiswal
8. Shri Datta Meghe
9. Dr. Jyoti Mirdha
10. Shrimati Jayshreeben Kanubhai Patel
11. Shri M.K. Raghavan
12. Shri Ratan Singh
13. Dr. Kirit Premjibhai Solanki
SECRETARIAT
Shri P.P.K. Ramacharyulu, Joint Secretary
Shri R. B. Gupta, Director
Shrimati Arpana Mendiratta, Joint Director
Shri Dinesh Singh, Deputy Director
WITNESSES
Representatives of Department of Health and Family Welfare
1. Shri P.K. Pradhan, Secretary
2. Shri K. Desiraju, Special Secretary
3. Dr. Vishwas Mehta, Joint Secretary
2. At the outset, the Chairman of the Committee welcomed the members of the Committee and
apprised them about the agenda of the meeting i.e. to hear the Secretary, Department of Health and
Family Welfare in light of suggestions received from various stakeholders/experts on the National
Commission for Human Resources for Health Bill, 2011. The Chairman apprised Members that on the
basis of inputs received so far on the Bill, he was of the opinion that the Bill was an effort to fully
centralize all power and jurisdiction pertaining to Health Education with the Union Ministry of Health
59

60
and Family Welfare. The proposed Bill while seeking to vest all powers and jurisdiction with the Union
endeavours to neglect State Governments. Public Health and Health Education have been kept under
the State List and Concurrent list respectively and any restriction on these powers is irrational because
implementation thereof is not only difficult but impossible without cooperation of the States. He further
informed that centralization of powers leads to dictatorship and arbitrariness and thus weakens the
framework of democracy and contrary to this, decentralization of powers strengthens democratic
roots and conventions. However, the proposed Bill seeks to entirely ignore interests and jurisdiction
of States and breaches autonomy of all existing Institutions of Health education thereby striking at
roots of their democratic functioning. Therefore, it would not be advisable to support the Bill in its
present shape. The Committee was also of the view that just because of a crunch of human resources
in the field of health services, powers and jurisdiction of States cannot be encroached upon, nor could
the Committee afford to dilute the autonomy and democratic functioning of institutions and institutes
engaged in the field of health education. The Committee underlined the fact that meeting the shortage
of human resources would not be possible without the co-operation of States. He was, therefore, of
the view that the Government may be asked to withdraw the Bill and introduce a revised Bill ensuring
protection of interests of the States and their participation. The revised Bill should also ensure the
democratic functioning and autonomy of the medical institutions while fixing their accountability.
Department of AYUSH should also be brought under the purview of the Bill instead of setting up of
a separate Commission. The proposed Commission should be an independent and autonomous regulator.
A mechanism should be put in place to ensure its accountability. Accordingly, appropriate recommendations
should be made in the report.
3. The Committee then heard the views of the Secretary and other officials, Department of Health
and Family Welfare on the proposed Bill to seek clarifications in the light of suggestions/comments
received from various stakeholders/experts and evidence tendered by a number of stakeholders. During
his deposition, the Secretary stated that his Ministry had received 53 memoranda from the Rajya Sabha
Secretariat containing views and suggestions by different organizations and individuals and the comments
thereon had been furnished. The Secretary highlighted the fact that the dominant view that emerged
from the memoranda furnished to the Committee and evidences tendered before it was that there was
an urgent need for reforms in the health education sector. He also emphasized the fact that AYUSH
should be kept outside the ambit of the Bill as bringing it under a single regulatory body may result
in AYUSH being relegated to an ancillary stream in the overall health care delivery system. Apprising
the Committee of the consultative process undertaken prior to drafting of the Bill, the Secretary stated
that when the formation of the overarching body was envisaged, a task force was constituted, which
prepared a report suggesting a model for National Commission for Human Resources for Health
(NCHRH). The report was also sent to the State Governments and other stakeholders and the draft
NCHRH Bill was put on the website of the Ministry, inviting comments and suggestion thereon. He
also stated that six regional consultations had also been held in which State Governments, Vice-
Chancellors, Principals of Medical Colleges, medical practitioners, academicians and representatives of
regional IMA had participated. He informed the Committee that the Government was open to increasing
the representation of State Governments in the National Commission, Board and Committee. He
impressed upon the Committee that the Bill would not usurp any powers that currently vest with the
State Governments. The registration with the State Councils and the National Councils, bar of
jurisdiction, provision for appellate authority, status of the courses run by National Board of Examinations,
formation of additional National Councils etc. also figured in the discussion.
4. During the course of the meeting, the members raised queries, some of which were answered
by the witnesses. The Committee directed the witness to send written replies to queries which
remained unanswered, within a week’s time.
5. A verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
6. The Committee then adjourned at 10.45 
A.M.
