other funds retirement, voluntary retirement, gratuity and other benefits
as he would have held under the existing Council if its undertaking
has not vested in the Commission and shall continue to do so as an
officer or, as the case may be, other employee of the existing Council

188
or until the expiry of a period of six months from the appointed day
if such officer or other employee opts not to continue to be the
officer or other employee of the existing Council within such period
and the Commission may transfer any officer or other employee or the
services of such officers or other employees as it may deem fit, to
the Board, Committee or the National Council.
It further provides that where an officer or other employee of
the existing Council opts under sub-clause ( 1) not to be in employment
or service of the Commission, such officer or other employee shall be
deemed to have resigned.
It also provides that notwithstanding anything contained in the
Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 or in any other law for the time being
in force, the transfer of the services of any officer or other employee
of the existing Council or the National Board of Examinations, as the
case may be, to the Commission shall not entitle such officer or other
employee to any compensation under proposed legislation or under any
other law for the time being in force and no such claim shall be
entertained by any court, tribunal or other authority and the officer
and other employees who have retired before the appointed day from
the service of the existing Council or the National Board of Examinations,
as the case may be, and are entitled to any benefits, rights or
privileges shall be entitled to receive the same benefits, rights or
privileges from the Commission.
It also provides to that the provident fund or the gratuity fund
of the existing Council or the National Board of Examinations, as the
case may be, and any other bodies created for the welfare of officer
or other employees would continue to discharge their functions in the
Commission as was being done hitherto in the existing Councilor or
the National Board of Examinations, as the case may be, and any tax
exemption granted to the provident fund or the gratuity fund would
continue to be applied to the Commission.
It also provides that notwithstanding anything contained in the
proposed legislation or in the Companies Act, 1956 or in any other law
for the time being in force or in the regulations of the existing Council
or the National Board of Examinations, as the case may be, the
President or Vice-President or member or the trustees, executive
trustee or any other person entitled to manage the whole or substantial
part of the business and affairs of the existing Councilor or the
National Board of Examinations, as the case may be, shall not be
entitled to any compensation against the Commission for the loss of
office or for the premature termination of any contract of management
entered into by him with the Commission.
Clause 119.—This clause provides for transitory provisions.
It provides that notwithstanding the repeal of the Indian Nursing
Council Act, 1947, the Pharmacy Act, 1948, the Dentists Act, 1948
and the Indian Medical Council Act, 1956, the educational standards,
requirements and other provisions of the Indian Nursing Council Act,

189
1947, the Pharmacy Act, 1948, the Dentists Act, 1948 and the Indian
Medical Council Act, 1956 and the rules and regulations made thereunder
shall continue to be in force and operate till new standards are
specified under the proposed legislation or the rules and regulations
made thereunder. However, anything done or any action taken as
regards the educational standards and requirements under the enactments
under repeal and the rules and regulations made thereunder shall be
deemed to have been done or taken under the corresponding provisions
of the proposed legislation and shall continue in force accordingly
unless and until superseded by anything done or by any action taken
under the proposed legislation and all such courses that are being
conducted by the National Board of Examinations shall continue till the
Central Government on the advice of the Commission notifies the
constitution of the Board under the proposed legislation.
It further provides that the Central Government may, if
considered necessary by notification, take such appropriate measures
as may be necessary for smooth transition of repealed Councils to the
corresponding new Councils and the existing National Board of
Examination to the Board under the proposed legislation.
Clause 120.—This clause relates to resolution of difference of
opinion by Joint Mechanism. This clause provides that in case of any
difference of opinion with the National Commission for Higher Education
constituted under the Higher Education and Research Act, 2011, the
same may be referred to the Joint Mechanism constituted under
section 52 of the Higher Education and Research Act, 2011 for
resolution of such difference of opinion in accordance with the provisions
of that Act.

190
FINANCIAL MEMORANDUM
Clause 3 of the Bill provides for establishment of the National
Commission for Human Resources for Health consisting of a
Chairperson; four whole-time members; and eight part-time members
to be appointed, by the Central Government. Sub-clause ( 3) of clause
6 of the Bill provides that the salary and allowances payable to and
other terms and conditions of service of the Chairperson and other
Members shall be such as may be prescribed.
2. Sub-clause ( 1) of clause 15 of the Bill provides for the
appointment of a Chief Executive Officer of the Commission, not
below the rank of Additional Secretary to the Government of India,
who shall be the Member-Secretary of the Commission. Sub-
clause ( 5) of said clause provides that salaries and allowances and
other terms and conditions of service of the Chief Executive Officer
and other officers and employees of the Commission shall be such as
may be prescribed.
3. Clause 23 of the Bill provides for constitution of a Board to
be known as the ‘National Board for Health Education’ consisting of
a President; two whole-time members; and four part-time members to
be appointed by the Central Government.
4. Sub-clause ( 1) of clause 28 of the Bill provides for
appointment of an Executive Director to be appointed by the Board.
Sub-clause ( 2) thereof provides for appointment other officers and
employees by the Board with the previous approval of the Commission.
Sub-clause (4) thereof provides that the salaries and allowances payable
to, and the other terms and conditions of service of the other officers
and employees of the Board shall be such as may be specified by
regulations made by the Commission.
5. Clause 34 of the Bill provides for constitution of a Committee
to be known as the National Evaluation and Assessment Committee
consisting of a President; two whole-time members; and four part-
time members to be appointed by the Central Government Sub-
clause (3) of clause 36 provides that the salary and allowances payable
to and other terms and conditions of service of the President and
other members of the Committee shall be such as may be specified
by regulation made by the Commission.
6. Sub-clause ( 1) of clause 39 of the Bill provides for
appointment of an Executive Director to the National Evaluation and
Assessment Committee by the Central Government Sub-clause ( 2)
thereof provides for appointment of other officers and employees by
the Committee with the previous approval of the Central Government.
Sub-clause ( 3) provides that the salaries and allowances payable to,
and the other terms and conditions of service of the Executive Director
190

191
and other officers and employees of the Committee shall be such as
may be specified by regulations made by the Commission.
7. Sub-clause ( 1) of clause 41 read with the First Schedule
provides for constitution of new National Councils, namely, the Medical
Council of India for medicine; the Dental Council of India for dentistry;
the Nursing Council of India for nursing; and the Pharmacy Council
of India for pharmacy. Sub-clause ( 2) thereof provides for constitution
of the Paramedical Council of India for the disciplines of paramedics.
Sub-clause ( 1) of clause 46 provides for composition of National
Councils.
8. Sub-clause ( 1) of clause 52 of the Bill provides for
appointment of a Registrar to every National Council by the Central
Government. Sub-clause ( 2) thereof provides appointment of other
officers and employees by the National Council with the prior approval
of the Central Government. Sub-clause ( 3) provides that salaries and
allowances payable to, and the other terms and conditions of service
of the other officers and employees of the National Council shall be
such as may be specified by regulations made by the National Council.
9. Sub-clause (1) of clause 53 of the Bill provides for constitution
of State Medical Council for medicine; the Dental Council for dentistry;
the Nursing Council of the State for nursing; the Pharmacy Council
for pharmacy; and the Paramedical Council for paramedics.
10. Clause 75 of the Bill provides that the Central Government
may, after due appropriation made by Parliament by law in this behalf,
make to the Commission grants of such sums of money as the Central
Government may think fit, for being utilised for the purposes of this
Act. Sub-clause ( 1) of clause 76 provides for constitution of a fund
to be called the National Commission for Human Resource for Health
Fund in which all Government grants, fees and charges received by
the Commission; and all sums received by the Commission shall be
credited.
11. lt is estimated that there would be an expenditure of
approximately forty crore of rupees. Out of this, an amount of thirty-
nine crore of rupees would be towards recurring expenditure and one
crore of rupees would be towards non-recurring expenditure.
12. The Bill does not envisage any other expenditure of recurring
or non-recurring nature.

192
MEMORANDUM REGARDING DELEGATED LEGISLATION
Clause 111 of the Bill confers power upon the Central
Government to make rules for carrying out the purposes of the Bill.
The matters with respect to which rules may be made has been set
out in sub-clause ( 2). The matters, inter alia, relate to ( a) the manner
of appointment of the Selection Committee under sub-section ( l) of
section 5; (b) the term of the Selection Committee under sub-
section ( 3) of section 5; ( c) the salary and allowances payable to and
other terms and conditions of the Chairperson and other members
under sub-section ( 3) of section 6; ( d) the salary and allowances and
other terms and conditions of service the Chief Executive Officer and
other officers and employees of the Commission under sub-section ( 4)
of section 15; ( e) such other functions to be performed by the
Commission under clause ( p) of sub-section ( 2) of section 16; ( f) any
other factors to be considered by the Committee and the Commission
while passing an order for approving or disapproving the scheme
under clause ( g) of sub-section ( 8) of section 17; ( g) the manner of
appointment of the chairperson and members of the Selection Committee
under sub-section ( 1) and their term under sub-section ( 4) of
section 25; ( h) the salary and allowances payable to and other terms
and conditions of service of the President and other members of the
Board under sub-section ( 3) of section 27; ( i) such other functions to
be performed by the Board under clause. ( ze) of sub-section ( 2) of
section 30; ( j) the number of persons to be appointed and the
qualifications and experience of the audit committee under sub-
section ( 1) of section 38; ( k) the procedure and the manner in which
the National Councils are to be constituted under sub-section ( 1) of
section 47; ( 1) the manner of conducting elections under sub-
section ( 4) of section 47; (
m) the manner of elections of the President
and Vice-President of each National Council under section 48; ( n)
such other functions which the National Council may perform under
clause ( g) of sub-section ( 2) of section 51; ( o) the manner in which
the National Register shall be maintained under sub-section ( 1) of
section 58; ( p) the manner in which the State Register shall be
maintained under sub-section ( 2) of section 58; ( q) the manner in
which a separate National Register shall be maintained under sub-
section ( 3) of section 58; ( r) the data capturing method by which the
National Councils shall maintain the register under sub-section ( 4) of
section 57; ( s) the manner in which the National Council shall update
and publish the Register under sub-section ( 5) of section 58; ( t) such
other purposes for which the National Register may be used under
sub-section ( 7) of section 58; ( u) the manner in which an application
may be made for entry of additional qualifications under section 66;
(v) the composition of and the procedure to be followed by the ethics
committee and the ethics appellate committee and terms and conditions
192

193
of such members under sub-section ( 4) of section 70; ( w) the form
in which an annual statement of accounts shall be maintained under
sub-section ( 1) of section 76; ( x) the time and the form in which an
annual report shall be furnished under sub-section ( 1) of section 78;
(y) the period within which appeal may be preferred to the Central
Government under sub-section ( 1) of section 99; ( z) the form of
application and the fees to be paid under sub-section ( 3), and
the procedure for disposing of an appeal under sub-section ( 4) of
section 99;
2. Clause 112 of the Bill confers power upon the National
Commission for Human Resources for Health to make regulations
consistent with the Act and the rules made thereunder to carry out the
purposes of the Act. The matters with respect to which regulations
may be made has been set out in sub-clause ( 2). The matters, inter-
alia, relate to ( a) the conduct of business at the meetings of the
Commission under sub-section ( 5) of section 13; ( b) the functions to
be discharged by the Chief Executive officer under sub-section ( 2) of
section 15; ( c) the continuous medical education and the courses
workshops and training activities under clause ( I) and such other
functions under clause ( p) of sub-section ( 2) of section 16; ( d) the
form, particulars and manner in which the scheme shall be made and
the fees, for submission of the scheme under sub-section ( 3) of
section 17; ( e) other functions in respect of which fees and charges
may be levied under section 22; ( f) the salaries and allowances payable
to, and other terms and conditions of service of the other officers and
employees of the Board under sub-section ( 4) of section 28; ( g) the
manner of conducting examination for entry to graduate, post graduate,
doctoral, post doctoral, super speciality or diploma courses and
fellowship examination by the Board under sub-section ( 1) of
section 31; ( h) the salary and allowances payable to and other terms
and conditions of service of the President and other members of the
Committee under sub-section ( 3) of section 36; ( i) the qualifications
and experience of, and the manner of evaluation and assessment of
health educational institutions by, the evaluation and assessment agencies
under sub-section ( 5) of section 37; (
j) the salary and allowances
payable to and other terms and conditions of service of the Executive
Director and other officers and employees of the Committee under
sub-section ( 3) of section 39; ( k) the fees and the conditions for re-
entry of name of the person under sub-section ( 2) of section 59;
(f) the period of suspension of the right of person to participate in
elections to council under sub-section ( 2) of section 62; ( m) the
circumstances and manner of removal of name of any person enrolled
by the National Council, or as the case may be, the State Council
under sub-section ( 1) of section 71; ( n) the manner of filing a
complaint with the State Council under sub-section ( 1) of section 72;
(o) the period for which the Board, Committee, National Councilor
State Council may engaged committees or experts, the qualifications of
such experts and the fees and remunerations to be paid under sub-
section ( 2) of section 95; ( p) the books of accounts and other
documents to be maintained by health educational institutions under

194
sub-section ( 4) of section 103; ( q) any other matter for which
provision may be made by regulations by the Commission under this
Act.
3. Clause 113 of the Bill confers power upon the State
Government to make rules for carrying out the purposes of the Bill.
The matters with respect to which rules may be made has been set
out in sub-clause ( 2). The matters, inter alia, relate to ( a) the manner
in which the State Councils are to be constituted and its composition
under sob-section ( 1) of section 53; ( b) the period of State Council
to continue under sub-section ( 5) of section 53; ( c) the manner of
election of the President and Vice-President of the State Council to be
elected by the members under sub-section ( 1) of section 54; ( d)
manner of enrolment of persons under sub-section ( 2) of section 57;
(e) the data capturing method by which the State Councils shall
maintain the register under sub-section ( 4) of section 57; ( f) the
manner in which the State Council shall update and publish the
Register under sub-section ( 5) of section 58; ( h) any other matter for
which provision may be made by rules by the State Government under
this Act.
4. Sub-clause ( 1) of clause 114 of the Bill requires that the rules
and regulations made under the proposed legislation shall have to be
laid before Parliament. Similarly, sub-clause ( 2) of the said clause
requires that the rules made by the State Government under the
proposed legislation shall have to be laid before the State legislature.
5. The matters in respect of which the rules and regulations
may be made are generally matters of procedure and administrative
detail and it is not practicable to provide for them in the Bill itself. The
delegation of legislative power is, therefore, of a normal character.

RAJYA SABHA
A
BILL
to consolidate the law in certain disciplines of health sector and promote human
resources in health sector and provide for mechanism for the
determination, maintenance, coordination and regulation of standards of
health education throughout the country to ensure adequate availability of
human resources in all States and for the said purpose to establish the
National Commission for Human Resources for Health and to supervise
and to regulate professional Councils in various disciplines of health sector
and for matters connected therewith or incidental thereto.
(Shri Ghulam Nabi Azad, Minister of Health and Family Welfare)

DEPARTMENT-RELATED PARLIAMENTARY
STANDING COMMITTEE ON HEALTH AND
FAMILY WELFARE INVITES SUGGESTIONS ON
THE NATIONAL COMMISSION FOR HUMAN
RESOURCES FOR HEALTH BILL, 2011
The National Commission for Human Resources for Health Bill, 2011, as introduced in the
Rajya Sabha, on 22 nd December, 2011 and pending therein, has been referred to the Department-
related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health and Family Welfare headed by Shri Brajesh
Pathak, M.P., Rajya Sabha for examination and report thereon within a period of three months.
2. The National Commission for Human Resources for Health Bill, 2011 seeks to consolidate the
law in certain disciplines of health sector and promote human resources in health sector and provide
for mechanism for the determination, maintenance, co-ordination and regulation of  standards of health
education throughout the country to ensure adequate availability of human resources in all the States.
3. In order to have wider consultations, the Committee has decided to invite written memoranda
containing suggestions/views/comments of individuals/institutions/organizations interested in the subject
matter of the Bill.
4. Those desirous of  submitting memoranda to the Committee may send two copies thereof,
either in English or Hindi, to Shrimati Arpana Mendiratta, Joint Director, Room No. 222, Rajya
Sabha Secretariat, Parliament House Annexe, New Delhi - 110001 [Tel: 23035428 (O),
Fax: 23012007]  within 30 days of  publication of  the advertisement. Besides, those who are desirous
of giving oral evidence before the Committee on the Bill are requested to indicate so, for consideration
of the Committee.
5. Copies of  the Bill may be obtained on  requisition from Shri Dinesh Singh, Assistant Director
(H&FW), Room No. 218, Rajya Sabha Secretariat, Parliament House Annexe, Sansad Marg, New
Delhi - 110001 or downloaded from the Rajya Sabha website 
rajyasabha.nic.in under the head ‘Committees’,
sub-head — ‘Bills with the Committee and Press release seeking opinions/suggestions from public’.
6. The memoranda submitted to the Committee will form part of the records of the Committee
and would be treated as strictly confidential and may not be made public as such an act would
constitute breach of privilege of the Committee.
Website: – rajyasabha.nic.in
E-mail: 
rs-chfw@sansad.nic.in

ANNEXURE-IV
Comments of MoHFW on the views/suggestions of the various stakeholders and the general public on the NCHRH Bill.
Memo Name of the Stakeholder Views/Comments of MoHFW
No.
12 3
1. Delhi Medical Association
• The Government is proposing for Centralisation of powers
by forming NCHRH and by taking away the autonomy of
all concerned Boards, Councils etc.
• MCI is an autonomous body and majority of its members
were elected and represented different States. The new
Commission is constituted of appointed Members.
• As per Clause 105(1) and (2), the Commission will be
bound by directions of the Central Government.
• There is no representation from professional organisations
like IMA or Health Universities.
• The current regulatory bodies regulate both education and pro-
fession practice within their domain and are over-burdened with
all regulatory functions. NCHRH in fact seeks to decentralise and
trifurcate the regulatory functions among three separate bodies
viz. NBHE, NEAC and National Councils.
• The existing jumbo size Councils comprises of nominated and

• Professional councils such as the Medical Council of India, the
Indian Nursing Council, Dental Council and the Pharmacy Council
have been set up by statutes of Parliament to regulate the
practice of their respective professions, including education.
However, many of these councils have attracted criticism for
their functioning, from health professionals', health administrators
and media. They have also drawn judicial censure on several
occasions. The existence of separate regulatory bodies, each
responsible for an important cadre of health workers, has also
failed to provide a synergistic approach to address the human
resources needs of the health sector in the country. There is
thus an urgent need for innovation in health related education
which encourages cross connectivity across disciplines and
categories of health workers.

• The main objective of the National Commission for Human
Resources in Health will be to provide an institutional framework
to promote availability of health care providers in all parts of the
country (doctors, nurses, dentists, pharmacists and paramedics)
to reduce shortages, standardize quality and bridge the uneven
distribution of the existing workforce. Accordingly, the
Commission’s immediate tasks will be to undertake a work
force study, formulate an action plan, and ensure inter-sectoral
coordination to promote availability of human resources in all
parts of the country.
• The Commission will be guided by a National Policy for Human
Resources for Health that will articulate a strategic vision and
normative framework for the creation of human resources for
health. This policy document, to be updated every five years,
will be developed by public health leaders representing State
and Central Governments through the Central Council for Health
and Family Welfare and shall be in the public domain.

ANNEXURE-VII
Views of Indian Nursing Council
Written Submissions to the NCHRH Bill 2011
(A) OBJECT AND PURPOSE OF THE NEW BILL
(i) The preamble of the bill envisages consolidating disciplines of health sector and to
promote human resources in health sector and to provide for mechanism for the
determination, maintenance, coordination and regulation of standards of health
education throughout the country to ensure adequate availability of human resources
in all states.
(ii) The bill envisages three different organs viz. NBHE, NEAC and NC to ensure that
the purpose and the object of the bill is fulfilled.
(B) “WHETHER THE OBJECT AND PURPOSE OF THE NEW BILL IS NOT ACHIEVED
THROUGH EXISTING LEGISLATION
(i) At present different Councils have been established by various enactments such as
Indian Nursing Council Act 1947, Dental Council Act and Pharmacy Council Act.
(ii) All these three statutory enactments are comprehensive in nature and are applicable
all across the country.
(iii) As perusal of the preamble of the three Acts would show that the purpose and
object of these existing Acts are the same as are stated in the proposed bill.
(iv) In so far as Nursing Profession in the country is concerned, the same is controlled
by Individual State Nursing Councils Act having been empowered with the
responsibility of recognizing various Nursing Institutions and for registration of
Nurses. These State Nursing Council Act are comprehensive in nature and the
State Councils have been vested with the responsibility to ensure that the Nursing
Health Institutions provide a good quality education to all. In case the proposed bill
is accepted then in that event the role of the State Governments to assist
formulations of various Nursing policies would be defeated. As a matter of fact the
various State Governments are stakeholders in the nursing education and its
development and hence the role of the State Governments/State Nursing Councils
cannot be whittled down since they alone are in a position to ensure that various
Government health policies are implemented to the needy citizens.
(v) In terms of Constitutional Provisions also education is in the concurrent list and
hence the framers of Indian Nursing Council Act 1947 have ensured that State
Governments and State Nursing Councils are also co-opted in the developing and
implementation of Nursing policies.
(vi) As per provisions of section 14 of Indian Nursing Council Act the power of
approval and recognition has also been conferred on the State Nursing Councils.

259
This power enables the State Governments/State  Nursing Councils to ensure that
the mandate of the Central enactment is implemented in all its force. As a stake
holder it is necessary to confer such powers so that there is no difficulty in
implementing the projects since the State Governments would also be answerable
for the successful implementation of health policies.
(C) OUR SUGGESTIONS
(i) The bill does not indicate as to what are the reasons for proceeding with such
massive exercise of amalgamating various councils which are admittedly performing
their public duties to the satisfaction of all concerned. It is also not indicated as
to how the amalgamation of the different Councils is going to help promote health
human recourses in the country. In the absence of any valid reasons, the presentation
and introduction of the above bill appears to be neither justified nor required.
(ii) At present the three statutory Councils viz. DCI, INC and PCI are performing
their functions in terms of their respective jurisdictions conferred by statutory
enactments. The respective Councils are discharging their mandate in terms of
statutory Act’s and as a matter of fact, the Counsels have been able to achieve the
goals set by the Government through various National Health Policies like National
Health Mission, National Health Rural Mission etc. In such circumstances no
useful purpose would be achieved by disbanding existing Statutory Councils and
amalgamating them into one big organization since after formation of the mammoth
organization it will be very difficult to manage and ensure completion of all health
goals.
(iii) At the time of constitution of these statutory bodies, the basic thinking was to
have professional councils having expertise in their respective health disciplines, so
that all the health disciplines have an equal opportunity for the growth and
development of each. It is not known as to how a Central Body with limited
professionals at the helm would be able to cater to the massive challenges facing
the health sector of the country.
(iv) The purpose and objects of creating separate statutory councils by the Government
of India is to decentralize the health sector so that the various specialist organizations
i.e. Medical, Dental, Nursing and Pharmacy etc. can develop at a rapid pace.
Introduction of one body though having separate councils would defeat the very
purpose of decentralization. At a time when decentralization is the buzzword,
resorting to having a one organization is a step backwards.
(v) A perusal of the Indian Nursing Council Act would show that the constitution of
the Members of the Council represent all the States of the Union. The basic
purpose is to ensure that all the States have equal opportunity to ensure adequate
health care delivery systems all across the country. The disbanding of the respective
councils will destroy the decentralized component of the council with the policies
being framed from one particular place little realizing the specific requirements of
each state.
(vi) Handling of smaller statutory councils is in the best interest of the country since
health sector requires a rapid growth. The formation of one central council for all
health related categories i.e. Nursing, Medical, Dental and Pharmacy would limit
the growth of Nursing sector as this sector is not given enough priority in terms
of finance, academic development growth of institutions etc.

260
(vii) Even otherwise it is simply not known as to why running statutory institutions are
sought to be disbanded especially when they too have been created after a through
application of mind and study. The factors which led to the creation of the separate
statutory councils still remain and therefore creating a new umbrella body is not
in the public interest since it would lead to uncertainty.
The very fact that different statutory councils have been incorporated having
limited jurisdictions was in keeping in view that at present the subject of “Health”
is in the concurrent jurisdiction. In the present enactments for example INC Act,
the State Governments are given powers to ensure that they too have a say in the
policy decisions relating to health education. Taking away the powers of the State
Governments in matters relating to health education would not be proper.

261
ANNEXURE-VIII
VIEWS OF DENTAL COUNCIL OF INDIA
It is stated that for the purpose of maintaining highest standards in imparting dental
education by the dental colleges/institutions in the country, the Dental Council of India (hereinafter
referred as DCI) was constituted as a statutory body under the provisions of Dentists Act, 1948.
It is the statutory and bounded duty of the Dental Council of India to take all such steps which
are required to be taken for  ensuring the maintenance of highest standards in dental education in
the country.
DCI as a statutory authority has the benefit of inputs from all relevant sections of the society
who are directly concerned with the dental education and practice of dentistry in this country. It is
stated that since its inception DCI is regulating dental education and training of dental practitioners
very smoothly and as of now there are 292 dental colleges all over the country out of which, 197
dental colleges are running PG degree and diploma course.
In view of the provisions of the Dentist Act, 1948, the constitution and composition of the
council has been done in a very democratic manner, there are representatives of all State Governments,
Universities, State Dental Councils, MCI as well as the Central Government. Even the Hon’ble
Supreme Court in catena of cases has accepted the role of DCI and held that the DCI is a statutory
expert authority constituted under an Act of Parliament, has been given the responsibility of discharging
the duty of maintenance of highest standard of dental education in the country.
NATIONAL COMMISSION FOR HUMAN RESOURCES
IN HEALTH BILL, 2011
The National Commission for Human Resources in Health Bill 2011, (hereinafter referred as
Health Bill) is being introduced to consolidate the law in the disciplines of health sectors and to
promote human resource in health sectors and provide for mechanism for the determination, maintenance,
co-ordination and regulation of standards of health education throughout the country to ensure
adequate availability of human resource in all States and for the said purpose to establish the National
Commission for Human Resources for Health to supervise and regulate professional Councils in
various disciplines of health sector.
COMMENTS/VIEWS ON NATIONAL COMMISSION FOR
HUMAN RESOURCES IN HEALTH BILL, 2011
• India is a federal country. The constitution of India has delegated and distributed powers
between Central and State. There are some sectors which are controlled by both Central
and State Governments. Health is one such important sector. Till date the health sector
is regulated by regulatory bodies (MCI, DCI, etc.) which has representatives from both
state and Central Governments as well as from the universities. The present bill proposes
to form a committee which will be totally under the control of the Central Government.
None of the States will have any role to play in policy issues relating to health. Thus the
bill is absolutely unconstitutional and against the spirit of federalism.
• Clause 100 and 101 page 34 says that the decision of the Commission cannot be
questioned in the court of law and the aggrieved person cannot seek legal remedies. This
261

262
makes the bill draconian and is against the principles of natural justice. It contravenes
upon the fundamental rights of an Indian citizen guaranteed by the Indian constitution.
• India is the worlds largest democratic country. Its government is formed by the people,
of the people and is for the people. The present bill proposes to form NCHRH, NBHE,
NEAC which will comprise of members totally appointed by Central Government. None
of the members of these bodies will be elected. This is absolutely an undemocratic
procedure and unacceptable. The issues related to the dissolution of the council and its
supersession by a supreme body needs to be viewed in the context of the constitutional
mandate of democracy. The underlying principal of democratic governance is that due
representation be brought in from the States and Centre and that the collective wisdom
of an elected body would then have built in checks and counterchecks to collectively
police, monitor and then regulate the profession. The Parliament while constituting an
expert council such as the DCI took due diligence and care that the said councils are free
from bureaucratic controls and not be just departments of the Government of India
without a voice or a conscience. The substitution of an elected body with due representation
from the State and the universities defeats and challenges the very principal of democratic
process.
• The DCI was created by an Act of parliament after a very long consideration and
deliberation by the stalwarts of the parliament in 1948 including Dr. R. Ahmed, the Father
of Dentistry in India. It has a fantastic democratic setup having representation from
almost all States of India. Such a democratic system is time tested and have proved to
be functioning extremely well and efficiently for such a long time.
• For complaints against individuals, such democratic institutions and statutory bodies
should not be destroyed. Therefore, we would request the Hon’ble members not to
stranglehold the Dental Council of India to death.
• Section 4j, page 54 (...to empower the Central Government to supersede commission,……)
and clause 115 (2) of the bill gives absolute autocratic power to the Central Government
to make the commission to dance to its tune. This again is against the very spirit of
democracy and thus unacceptable.
• The bill in its preamble says ….to supervise and regulate professional councils in various
disciplines of health sector……..But in reality it wants to dissolve all bodies and take
away all their powers and funds. This makes the bill extremely weak for it contradicts
its own principal.
• At present DCI has 70+ members and MCI had over 150 members regulating their
respective fraternity. Inspite of collectively having more than 230+ members both the
councils are finding it taxing to regulate all the teaching institutes. The present bill
proposes to form a commission consisting of one chairperson, 4 whole time members
and 8 part time members which will determine, maintain, co-ordinate and regulate the
standard of health education throughout the country. How 13 members will do the above
job is beyond our wildest imagination. This makes the bill illogical and absurd.
• The bill proposes to dissolve all the existing councils (Medical Council, Dental Council,
Pharmacy Council, Nursing Council, Paramedical Council etc.) and plans to form a
committee which aims to take over the function of the above mentioned councils. How
a single committee can perform such a Herculean task is not known, for this type of

263
challenge has not been taken by any existing developed nation in the world. Every
developed nation has got a separate body (medical, dental, nursing physiotherapy etc.)
comprising of its own professional members to take care of their own needs. India will
be the first country to have such a daring Committee.
• In this bill no road map has been formulated as to how the Government is going to
remove the shortage of manpower in Health sector by forming the Commission.
Professionals from other disciplines like Management Technology and Law were given
place in this Commission will further dilute the standard of education as they are not
technically experts in health sector.
• There is no provision in the Health Bill as to how the Government will ensure the
adequate availability of medical resource in all States to cater to the population. It is stated
that in our country, there is a need to regulate the hospitals to provide free medical
treatment to the population below the poverty line. However, the Health Bill is once again
silent on this aspect.
• According to the Health Bill, the role of the National Council will only  be to enroll and
monitor the individuals, maintain their registers and organize seminars only.
• The new council so proposed is an appointed body, which would necessarily be an
extension of the Government if not a department all together. It is difficult to visualize
as to what autonomy and representation by an appointed body could exercise. A proposal
to be vetted by 70 members in general body imposes a sense of discipline on the system.
An appointed body would in turn have no larger forum for brainstorming an issue and
would perhaps be arbitrary, dictatorial and empirical in its function with no inbuilt
controls. The other issue that emerges is that does the new council have a change in
function? To regulate higher education, imposition and enforcement of standards is a
must, and to that extent we do not see the NCHRH being able to change the mode or
context of inspections and recommendation for approval of institutions.
• The section 54 of  the Dentist Act, 1948 clearly looks at a remedial measure to appoint
an independent commission of  enquiry for any alleged misdemeanors or issues of
corruption. The justice Anil Dev Singh committee went through the structure and function
of the DCI and made specific recommendations, all of  which have since been implemented
save one, which was not, passed by the General Body. Would the newly appointed
council have any such built in measures for investigating issues of corruption or
malpractice or would be all powerful and above any such controls.
• The health education policies will keep changing with the change of power at the centre,
thus lacking consistency in the overall approach to health care resource management. If
any Government in future takes a wrong decision it will definitely jeopardize the whole
health care system. We all know that to spoil a jar of milk all it takes is a drop of a
lemon.
• Finally we will have to realize and think that:
The Bill advocates:
Selection instead of election

264
Autocracy instead of democracy
Dictation instead of Regulation
We are the world’s largest democracy. We have a democratic system which is a matter of
pride for us. Can we accept a bill which is totally against the very spirit of the Constitution,
a bill which is draconian in every nature, killing fundamental rights of its citizens. Can we
afford a bill which revokes 4 standing Acts, which have been created by visionary stalwarts
of our Constitution and have stood the test of time.
Therefore, I will request the Hon’ble Members not to strangle hold the fantastic setup of our
health system and maintain status quo maintaining and further strengthening the existing health
education system.

265
ANNEXURE-IX
VIEWS OF INDIAN MEDICAL ASSOCIATION
The Medical Council of India was formed by an Act of Parliament, IMC Act, 1956 and functioning
with nominated members from Central/State Governments, Health Universities and Elected members
through Registered Members of Medical Profession.
Dissolved in April, 2010 based on alleged corruption charges. The function of 130 members was taken
over by 7 handpicked persons as Board of  Governors with an assurance that MCI will be restored
within one year.
On completion of  one year, the Hon’ble Minister of Health and Family Welfare requested the Parliament
to accord extension to the BoG for 1 more year assuring the House that no further extension will be
sought. The number of  BoG members was brought down to 6 this time.
Third year NCHRH Bill, 2011 was introduced.
Medical and Health Allied Professionals of  the country vehemently opposed the Bill.
Some Members of  Parliament too opposed the Bill.
Two Chief Ministers of India objected to it in writing.
Bill was referred to Parliamentary committee on Health.
Why MCI was dissolved?
• MCI was dissolved in April, 2010 upon alleged corruption charges.
• Many heads of statutory bodies of India are facing corruption charges.
• However, the Institutions are not dissolved and are still functioning.
• Why should MCI singularly be dissolved?
• MoHFW repeatedly trying to usurp the autonomy of MCI.
• Supreme Court Committee in 2002 testified the need for autonomy of MCI.
Powers of MoHFW on MCI?
• Powers to form  Rules and notify them.
• Prior approval of Government is needed to notify regulations.
• Right to constitute Enquiry Committee.
• 37 Nominated members.
• To start new Medical colleges/Courses, MCI is only a Recommending Authority.
• Direct Control of  PG education rests with Medical Education Committee, 6 of whom are
nominated by the Government out of  a total of 9.
265

266
A perusal of  the existing provisions of Indian Medical Council Act, 1956 would categorically
bring out that the Government of India has substantial and reasonable control on the affairs
of the Medical Council of India.
MoHFW attempt to take over Autonomous MCI
In 2005, the then Union Health minister, Dr Anbumani Ramadoss, tried unsuccessfully to bring
in a legislation to dissolve the MCI and set up another council under the control of the Health
Ministry. After stiff opposition from Professional organisations, the draft Bill which was placed before
the cabinet, was referred to the Standing from Committee of Parliament on Health following the
intervention by the Prime Minister’s Office.
The Hon’ble Supreme Court in the year 2002 appointed an Ad-hoc Committee of three eminent medical
educationists including Dr. P.N. Tandon, Dr. Rangabhashyam and Dr. Mrs. S Kantha to oversee and
monitor the functioning of Medical Council of India for the purposes of enhancement of its credibility
and credence. The said Committee that justified the need of extension of a meaningful and
bonafide autonomy to the Medical Council of India.
IMA strongly opposes NCHRH Bill, 2011. Why?
The frame-work of the Bill seriously falls short of any evidence affirming at an honest attempt
to achieve its listed objectives.
This Bill does not assure of any improvement in quality of Health Care and accessibility.
Objective/Vision of the NCHRH Bill, 2011
• To consolidate the laws in certain disciplines of Health sector.
• To promote Human Resources in the health sector.
• To provide for mechanism for the determination, maintenance, coordination and regulation
of standards of health education throughout the country.
• To ensure adequate availability of human resources in all the states.
IMA’s Objections NCHRH Bill
• The purported object of the Bill to provide healthcare for all, is hidden from the
perspective of the individual regulatory bodies and is untenable to be considered enough
reason to bring in this Bill with sweeping powers.
• Classical example of colorable exercise of power and abuse of legislative process.
• The case for abolishing the existing institutions is unconvincing and by taking away the
powers of these autonomous councils, the Government loses credibility in the eyes of
people of India.

norms and Federalism.

NCHRH Bill Summarising………
Indian Medical Association does not accept NCHRH Bill 2011, for the undermentioned reasons:
• Democracy is lost to Autocracy:
o Representative character of  the Democratic institution is lost. Only appointed members
with indefinite tenure
Can the Indian Parliament become an Appointed Body?
• Federal Fabric of Indian Constitution is lost.
Right of States being usurped by the Central Government.
• Fundamental Right of Citizen is eroded.
o No legal remedy for Disputes
o Doctors engaging in other Profession is misconduct
o Registration with State Council is valid only for that state
• NCHRH Bill will pave the way for corruption.
External Evaluation Agency is a standing example.
• National Board of Health Education with 6 members  may not be able to control the
vast number of institutions and courses.
• Funds of Democratic Councils cannot be transferred to an Autocratic Body.
Decentralization is the way to better organization and achievements but NCHRH Bill will lead to
Centralization of powers leading to poor performance of the custodian of Medical Education which
will jeopardize tomorrows Health Care and Health science education of India
What is the way forward?
IMA’s Health Vision for India is:
To retain-the Autonomous Representative character of Medical and Allied Councils
functioning in a Transparent manner.
Solutions are:
• Restore the Medical and Allied Councils by conducting elections democratically
immediately
• Increase the number of elected members from the Indian Medical Register (IMR) pro-
rata, Professional Bodies and Health care professionals.
• Let the councils be observed keenly by the MoHFW using the powers vested with them
in a structured manner and adequate checks and balances.
It is pointed out that:-
1. The existing institutions have stood the test of time and have served the nation well.

269
2. They have a democratic structure and a representative character.
3. The plurality of the voices represented reflect the Federal nature of our Union.
4. The nominated/appointed NCHRH can never have either the legitimacy nor the wisdom of
the present institutions.

ANNEXURE-X
Views of Delhi Medical Association
1. Decentralization is a slogan of the Union Government for better organization and achievements.
But it is unfortunate to learn that the benign Government is proposing for centralization of powers by
forming National Commission and by taking away the autonomy of all the concerned Boards, Councils
and other wings of the Union Government for health.
2. The Medical Council of India, constituted by Indian Medical Council Act, 1956, has been an
autonomous body regulating medical education in the country. As the majority of the members were
elected and represented different states, they were able to give unbiased opinion regarding medical
institutions and medical courses. This new Commission is constituted by Central Government appointed
members. They will act like a department of union Ministry of health and family welfare and will not
be able to give unbiased opinion.
3. As per the clause 105(1) and (2), the Commission, the Board, committee or the National
Council will be bound by the directions of the Central Government. In other words, the Commission,
the Committee and the Council will execute the orders given by the Central Government. In the
circumstances, it will not be possible for the Commission, Board, committee or Council to provide fair
assessment of the medical education or health care delivery.
4. There is no provision for the representation from the professional organizations, like Indian
Medical Association or Health Universities in the Commission, Board or Council, shunning the voice
of the health care providers.
5. Sec. 100 which bars challenging of removal of name from state register should be deleted. It
is unconstitutional and is against the fundamental principles of constitution. No law can bar the citizen
of India from seeking a judicial recourse for getting relief against the decision of a regulatory body.
Further a single body can’t be investigating, prosecuting, judging and then final super punishing
authority whose decision is un-challengeable in the court of Law. It is against natural justice.
6. The bill fails to talk about Rehabilitation Council of India Act 1991, Delhi Council for
Physiotherapy and Occupational Therapy Act : 1997, Maharashtra State Council for Occupational
Therapy and Physiotherapy Act-2002,  Gujarat State Council for Occupational Therapy and Physiotherapy
Act-20ll, the Tamil Nadu State Physiotherapy order etc.
7. Modern medicine is not defined properly it is defined only as medicine. Health professionals
are not properly, defined to discharge their duties. Without defining properly the objectives of the bill
will not be accomplished.
8. Section 2.(u) of the Bill defines modern medicine as provided under the Indian Medical Council
Act-l956. The definition of modern medicine when defined in this bill should clearly specify it as
modern medicine as not as medicine. Hence the word “medicine” at the beginning of the definition
should be replaced as “modern medicine”.
9. Similarly the words “medical practitioner” in section 2(t), should be replaced with the words
“modern medical practitioner”

271
10. The Bill clearly defines modern medicine under section 2(u) and also defines modern medical
practitioner under section 2(t). The Bill has completely failed in defining other heath professional
branches and professionals clearly and squarely. The Bill fails to Define Dentistry, Dentists, Nursing,
Nurses, Pharmacy, Pharmacists, Paramedics and Paramedical Personal.
The Delhi Medical Association totally rejects the proposed “National Commission for Human
Resources for Health Bill, 2011 for the following reasons:-
(1) Supervision and regulation is not by dissolving
The Bill in its preamble says “…..to supervise and regulate professional Councils in various disciplines
of health sector” Page (1), However subsequently it dissolves all the existing Councils and takes away
all their duties and fund.
(2) Autonomy is lost
(i) In the statement of objects and reasons 4(j), page 54 says “to empower the Central
Government to supersede Commission, Board, Committee or National Council”. It gives
autocratic power to Central Government to make the Commission to dance to its tunes.
(ii) And also clause 115 (2) page says “If the Commission or Board or Committee or National
Council as the case may be fails (or) neglects to comply with such order, Central
Government may make the regulations (or) amend (or) revoke the regulations made by
the Commission, ………… as the Central Government thinks fit.
(iii) National Commission, Board, Committee all will have only appointed or nominated members
by the Central Government and no elected person will be there.
(iv) Clause 12 says Central Government may by order remove from office the chair person
or any members of the Commission.
(3) Reduce the shortage and uneven distribution?
The statement of object and reason for the bill section (2) page 53 says “………. to reduce shortage,
standardize quality and bridge the uneven distribution of existing work force in the health sector”. No
roadmap how the Government is going to reduce the shortage of manpower by forming this
Commission is said without statistical evidence based input. Using attractive words will not solve the
problem. No need to dissolve the existing health Councils to achieve this purpose. Health is a State
subject and resources cannot be redistributed by Central Government.
“There is acute shortage of water and electricity in various parts of country. Will the Central
Government constitute the Commission to make even distribution?”
(4) Do we need more specialist (or)  Family Physicians
Prime Minister of India said India needs more Family physicians and efforts will be taken to increase
the importance of  Family physician
The bill do not talk about this but says 4 (c) page 53 “…… to ensure uniform augmentation of trained
specialist and superspecialist” as its priority.
(b) Fund Transfer is the vision?
4 (g) says “to constitute a fund to be called the National Commission for Human Resources for
Health Fund” and 4 (h) says to transfer the assets, liabilities, rights, duties etc., of the existing
Council to the Commission. There are different Councils with age old registered members and their

272
fees as assets and transferring them to common pool and vesting the authorities with Central
Government appointed Commission is not acceptable. Funds of democratic Councils cannot be
transferred to autocratic body.
(5) What about the Ayush Council and Rehabilitation Council
4(k) page 54 says “to repeal the INC Act, 1947, Pharmacy Act, 1948, Dentist Act, 1948 and IMC
Act, 1956 and dissolve the respective Council. However we are having AYUSH Council dealing with
Health Care and Rehabilitation Council of India Act, 1991. What will happen to this Council?
(6) Audit and Accounting
Clause 77 says even the accounts of State Councils will be audited only by the CAG of Central
Government. State will have no role on this.
(7) Is the bill is above the existing laws?
Clause 100 & 101 (Page 34) says the decisions of the Commission cannot be questioned in the court
of law and the agreed persons cannot seek legal remedies.
(8) Doctor doing any other occupation is misconduct?
The seventh schedule in continuation of section 68 in Part 1(10) (page 50) says engage in any
business (or) occupation other than health profession is a misconduct. This prohibits all career
opportunities for health professionals.
(9) No elected members from Professional Associations in. National Commission
(1) National Commission the proposed supreme body with vested powers will have no elected
members and State representatives to represent their needs and demands
(2) Professionals from other discipline of Management technology and law are given place in this
Commission will pave way for dilution and nepotism
(3) It is unfortunate in a democratic country elected representative Council is brought under appointed
non democratic body
(10) Common Entrance Examination by National Board of Health Education
(i) The proposed Common Entrance Examination by National Board, without providing
common syllabus for +2 exams, will affect  the students not having opportunity to study
CBSE syllabus. This will imbibe the social fabric and reservation systems in each State.
Many students study + 2  in their regional language. So entry level common entrance is
not a viable option (Clause 31 page 15)
(ii) Screening test for foreign graduates is welcome. But power of commission to exempt
persons from appearing in screening test will jeopardize the system (Clause 33 (4) page 16)
(iii) Power of National Board for Health Education to give equivalency certificate with foreign
National Course is objectionable.
(iv) The role of democratic National Medical Council responsible for Medical Education is
taken away and handed over to 7 members of National Board of Health Education. It is
strange no representative of Council (or) State is included in the Board
(v) Distance education mode without hands on training is a dangerous move for licensing a
human handler

273
(vi) The vision to promote cross disciplinary programmes in health sector will enhance
quackery (clause 30 (z) )
(11) National evaluation and Assessment Committee
(1) With only 6 members and quorum of 2 will be a mess to evaluate and take decision on the nearly
20,000 health institutions in all disciplines (Clause 37 e).
(2) Using external evaluation agency will increase discrepancy
(12) National Council
(i) The vision and objective with which the National Councils constituted are thrown in to
dustbin and only the power of registration is restored with Councils. State Councils are
made mere the branches of Central Council.
(ii) The membership pattern is unacceptable.
(i) Instead of one member for each state, one member for 5000 doctors must be
emphasized.
(13) Registration in State Council valid only for the state
In contrary to the existing practice as per clause 57 (4),  a candidate who have registered in State
Council can practice only in the State of registration and only when you register in the National
Council can practice anywhere in India. This will negativate State Councils and no one will come
forward to register in the State Council.
- MEDICAL EDUCATION IS A SPECIFIED AREA.
- IN SHORT NCHRH IS OF THE GOVERNMENT BY THE GOVERNMENT AND FOR
THE GOVERNMENT AND NOT FOR THE HEALTH PROFESSION AND EDUCATION.
- NCHRH WILL DEFINITELY AFFECT THE HEALTH EDUCATION AND HEALTH
SCENARIO OF INDIA.
- INDEPENDENT COUNCIL IS THE NEED OF THE MEDICAL PROFESSION.

Dr. Harish Gupta Dr. V .K. Kohli
President, DMA Hony. State Secretary, DMA

ANNEXURE-XI
Views of Indian Association of Physiotherapy (Mangalore), Karnataka
Honorable Member
Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health and Family Welfare
Respected Sir/Madam,
Sub: Submitting Memorandum to create Physiotherapy Council of India for Physiotherapy de
novo as per section 2 (i) and 2 (k) in the proposed  Bill No. LIX of 2011 the National Commission
for Human Resources for Health Bill, 2011
We must humbly thank you for giving us the opportunity to present the view of  the Indian Association
of Physiotherapists regarding “The National Commission for Human Resource in Health Bill 2011”. We
hereby submit the memorandum.
The IAP appreciates the  effort of the Health Ministry to bring all disciplines of  health sector in the
proposed bill to coordinate and regulate the standards of health education throughout the country.
In a letter dated 30th September, 2011 from Union Health Minister to the Union Finance Minister
(Annex 1) regarding formation of Central Independent ‘Physiotherapy Council Honorable Health
Minister mentioned that Physiotherapy stream will be included in the NCHRH Bill. We were really
worried to see no mention of  word Physiotherapy in the NCHRH Bill of 2011.
We would like the kind attention of your goodself that, at present there are 296 colleges offering
under-graduate (B.P.T) Physiotherapy programme consisting of 4½  years and 116 colleges offering
Masters in Physiotherapy (M.P.T) with 2 years duration. Ph.D programme in Physiotherapy is
offered at 6 colleges and 5 universities in our country. There are 63,963 qualified physiotherapists
practicing all over India. Till now we have no Central Council for Physiotherapy to regulate the
profession.
We would like to bring to your kind attention the Planning Commission of India Eleventh Five year
Plan (2007-2012) on Health and Family Welfare (Sec 3.1.104 on Accreditation and Regulation Page 84
and 85) stating that “In the field of paramedical education, priority will be given for establishment of
National Para Medical Council as an apex body to determine standards and to ensure uniform
enforcement throughout the country. On similar lines, councils for physiotherapy and occupational
therapy should also be established” (Annex. 2).
The above mention of the Planning commission clearly delineates Paramedics from Physiotherapists.
In the Thirty-First report on Paramedical and Physiotherapy Central Councils Bill, 2007 (Presented to
the Rajya Sabha on 21
st October, 2008 and laid on the table of Lok Sabha on 21 st October, 2008)
discussed elaborately about the status and scope of Physiotherapy practice as following. (Annex. 3)

275
Clause 8.2 “Physiotherapy being considered a discipline distinct from paramedical disciplines finds a
specific mention in the title (Paramedical and Physiotherapy Central Councils Act, 2007)”
Clause 9.36 Committee’s attention was also drawn to the parallel position of  Naturopathy and Yoga
when compared with physiotherapy as both were based on physical and psychosomatic methods of
diagnosis and treatment, with both claiming benefit to the patients in a drugless manner. It was argued
that both Naturopathy and Yoga are granted equal status along with Ayurveda under AYUSH. Thus,
physiotherapy also deserved to be granted an independent status. Clause 9.46 “The fact that physiotherapy
education over the years has made significant advancements and has evolved as a distinct profession
seems to be well established. This is strengthened by the considered opinion of  Ministry of  Law that
physiotherapy profession should not be equated with the paramedical professions”
Clause 9.47 “The Committee also takes note of the fact as mentioned by the representative of the
Ministry that in USA, physiotherapy profession has reached a stage where these professionals can
practise independently. Not only this, in some of  the international Acts, it has been specifically provided
that physiotherapists having the required experience can give physiotherapy treatment without a
referral. These Acts also confer upon a physiotherapist the right to practise with or  without referral
governed by the circumstances of  the case.”
Clause 25.6 The Committee feels  that all the allied health professionals including physiotherapists and
occupational therapists play a crucial role in the field of  medicine and physical rehabilitation. The
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that their legitimate interests should be taken care of  and
their existing pay structure may be revised according to their qualifications and duration of the course
they have to put in before entering into a Government job. The findings and documentation of this
committee are in favour strong of  an independent status and council for Physiotherapists.
We would like to  bring to your kind notice that Independent State Councils for Physiotherapists are
existing in  the state of Delhi, Maharashtra and Gujarat.
The World Health Organization (WHO) has classified physiotherapists in professional group (ISCO
Code 1264) and paramedical professionals have been classified in a separate entity (ISCO code 2240).
Ref:WHO:http://www.who.int/hrh/statistics/Healt_workers_classification.pdf) (Annex . 4)
We Physiotherapists being one of the pre-eminent healthcare professional would like to request you to
look into our following demands:–
1. Facilitate the formation of Physiotherapy Council of India for Physiotherapy de novo .
2. Not to include Physiotherapy in Paramedical section as they are completely different.
3. The members of the Physiotherapy council should be only from  Physiotherapy stream including
Chairman, Vice-Chairman and Registrar so that 2/3
rd members of the council are physiotherapists.
4. The members of the council must be elected and the provision of election must be there in
the Bill.
Thanking you and hoping for your valuable cooperation to solve our above mentioned professional
concerns.

ANNEXURE-XII
Views of Amar Jyoti Institute of Physiotherapy
(University of Delhi)
Currently the following councils have been proposed in the Health Bill, 2011:
• Medicine under Medical Council of India
• Dentistry under Dental Council of India
• Nursing under Indian Nursing Council
• Pharmacy under Pharmacy Council of India
• Paramedical under Paramedical Council of India
In the draft bill National Council for Health Resources for health Bill, 2009, there were 7 departments
which included Physiotherapy also. But in the current bill the same has not been included which will
lead to a lot of ambiguity about the status of the profession.
The above mentioned councils in the proposed 2011 Bill may not be able to justify regulation of all
healthcare professions specially Physiotherapy. Physiotherapy as a profession has grown tremendously
in the last few decades and the country has witnessed increased demand of the services of the
Physiotherapists. As a result, a large number of Physiotherapy clinics and departments in Government
and non-Government hospitals exist throughout the country like in many developed nations such as
US, UK, Australia etc. (copies regarding the profession of physiotherapy in some countries attached).
Physiotherapy is an extensive 4½  year Bachelor course now in India with a post-graduation in
specialties like musculoskeletal, neurological rehabilitation, sports injuries, cardiopulmonary rehabilitation,
community based rehabilitation. Many Physiotherapists in academia are also doctorates. The current
curriculum in many government Physiotherapy institutions is as elaborate as any other medical
profession.
The Parliamentary Committee Report 2008 (Department-Related Parliamentary Standing Committee on
Health and Family Welfare: Thirty-first report on Paramedical and Physiotherapy Central Councils Bill,
2007, presented to the Rajya Sabha on 21
st October, 2008, laid on the table of Lok Sabha on 21 st
October, 2008) also recommended the formation of a separate council for Physiotherapy. Kindly refer
to the following content of the report.
Excerpt from the Parliamentary Committee Report 2008
9.46 The Committee has given deep thought to all the views and opinions aired before it by all
concerned. V oluminous material relating to both national and international arena placed before it has
also received full attention of the Committee. The fact that physiotherapy education over the years has
made significant advancements and has evolved as a distinct profession seems to be well established.
This is strengthened by the considered opinion of Ministry of Law that physiotherapy profession
should not be equated with the paramedical professions.
9.47 The Committee also took an opportunity to go through the definition of physiotherapy as
given in different State Acts of USA, Canada, New Zealand etc. The Committee did not notice the

277
specific use of words ‘medically directed’ in the definition of ‘physiotherapy’ given in these Acts. The
Committee would also like to point out that a definition should only describe the profession enumerating
its different characteristics and not its administrative part. The Committee also takes note of the fact
as mentioned by the representative of the Ministry that in USA physiotherapy profession has reached
a stage where these professionals can practice independently. Not only this, in some of the international
Acts, it has been specifically provided that physiotherapists having the required experience can give
physiotherapy treatment without a referral. These Acts also confer upon a physiotherapist the right to
practice with or without referral governed by the circumstances of the case. The Committee would
also like to point out that the analogy of including the words ‘medically directed’ should have been
followed in the definitions of medical Laboratory technology and radiology technology also. The
Committee is, however, surprised to note that definition of these two professions to be governed by
separate Central Councils is completely ignored by making a mere mention of these two professions
under the definition of ‘paramedical’. This confirms Committee’s apprehension that the words ‘medically
directed’ have been deliberately used defeating the very basis of defining a profession.
• We would also like to bring to your kind notice the following definitions of physiotherapy as
laid down by already existing separate state councils of Physiotherapy.
(i) According to  the Delhi Council for physiotherapy and Occupational Therapy Act, 1997.
“Physiotherapy” means physiotherapeutic system of medicine which includes examination,
treatment, advice and instructions to any persons preparatory to or for the purpose of or
in connection with movement dysfunction, bodily malfunction, physical disorder, disability,
healing and pain from trauma and disease, physical and mental conditions using physical
agents including exercise, mobilization, manipulation, mechanical and electrotherapy, activity
and devices or diagnosis, treatment and prevention.
(ii) According to the Maharashtra State Council for Occupational Therapy and Physiotherapy
Maharashtra Act, 2004.
“Physiotherapy” means a branch of modern medical science, which includes examination,
assessment, interpretation, physical diagnosis, planning and execution of treatment and
advice to any person for the purpose of preventing, correcting, alleviating and limiting
dysfunction, acute and chronic bodily malfunction including life saving measures via chest
physiotherapy in the intensive care units, curing physical disorders or disability promoting
physical fitness, facilitating healing and pain relief and treatment of physical and psycho-
somatic disorders through modulating physiological and physical response using physical
agent, activities and devices using exercises, mobilization, manipulations, therapeutic ultra-
sound, electrical and thermal agents and electrotherapy for diagnosis, treatment and prevention.
As per WCPT (World Confederation of  Physical Therapists), Physiotherapists are qualified and
professionally required to:
• Undertake a comprehensive examination/assessment/evaluation of the patients
• Formulate a diagnosis, prognosis, and plan Physiotherapy management
• Provide consultation within their expertise and determine when patients/clients need to be
referred to another healthcare professional
• Make recommendations for self management
It is interesting to note that in spite of having state councils for Physiotherapy in some states, the
profession lacks a Central council and our professional bodies throughout the country have been
requesting the same.

278
Considering that Physiotherapy cannot be regulated by Medicine, dentistry, Pharmacology and Nursing
councils, it is being assumed that either physiotherapy will be included under the paramedical council
or a separate council will be formed as per Clause2(k) and 41 (2) after the notification of the act.
Therefore it is requested that a separate council for Physiotherapy be included (as per section 2 (k)
& 41(2) before the inception of the National Commission which will not only help the profession
grow in India but will also help the healthcare system of India in following global trends of
healthcare.
Item No.2
The National Board of  Health Education (Chapter III) 24.3 and 24.4  states that the members of
this board should have post graduate degree in medicine and medical education. But in our opinion,
the responsibility vested upon this board will also require them to formulate and implement policies
regarding education in other areas of healthcare.
It is recommended that the Board has members from other fields special1y Physiotherapy in order
to regulate and improve education in the field of Physiotherapy.
OR
It is recommended that sub boards be constituted under the National board for maintaining and
improving the standards of education for areas of healthcare such as Physiotherapy which cannot
be included in any of the proposed councils.
Item No.3
Section 8. ( 1) States that “The educational qualifications granted by any health educational institution
in India which are included in the Second Schedule or the Third Schedule or the Fourth Schedule or
the Fifth Schedule or the Sixth schedule, as the case may be, shall be recognised qualifications of the
respective discipline of  health for the purposes of this Act”.
However, the schedules I-VI do not include educational requirements of various other disciplines which
cannot be included under paramedical council such as Physiotherapy, Homeopathy, Unani etc. Madam,
we would like to reiterate that Physiotherapy education has changed drastically in the last few years
not only in India but globally in order to match the trends of Physiotherapy practice. Graduate, post
graduate and doctorate courses are being offered in Physiotherapy in many government universities of
India and their curriculum is quite exhaustive.
it is recommended that Physiotherapy education be included separately in the schedules (I-VI)
mentioning the education requirements of different healthcare professions.

Organization) and ILO (International Labor Organization) are two International Organizations at global
level of repute. These organizations provide Physiotherapy professionals a Independent status and right
to act as a first hand practitioners in health care delivery system throughout the world.

ANNEXURE-XV
Views of All India Dental Hygienist Association
Request regarding favourable consideration of our genuine and justified Demand for the welfare
and betterment of Dental Hygiene profession and professional in the interest of Public Health care.
1. To make separate Dental Hygienist Council for the inclusion of Dental Hygienist profession
separately in the proposed “National Commission of Human Resources for Health Bill, 201 1”.
2. Provision of Higher Education for Dental Hygienist
(a) Bachelor of Dental Hygiene
(b) Master of Dental Hygiene
(c) Ph.D in Dental Hygiene
(d) Post Doctoral in Dental Hygiene
(e) Foreign Dental Hygienist Qualification.
3. Separate Department of oral and Dental Hygiene in each and every Government/Private
Hospitals and in Parliament House Annexe, New Delhi, Secretariat of state assembly like
Physiotherapist, in the interest of public health care, Hon’ble Ministers and Member of
Parliament/Member of state assembly.
4. Time bound Promotion Avenue after every 5 years of the service to the next post and pay
scale,
5. To rectify clause 60 (2) of proposed NCHRH Bill, 2011.
6. To make provision for The Dental Hygienist for lateral entry in BDS courses. (Regular/
Correspondence)

Dr. Chawla in his additional written submissions highlighted certain other anomalies observed
in certain clauses of the Bill. He was of the view that the Bill was silent on the manner in which part
time members of the Commission are be appointed, whether these would be from academic institutes
or those m practice. He also had the doubt as to whether the Chairman would be part time or full
time. The Commission should also  ensure maintenance of standards of human resource. The President
of National Board of Health Education should have at least 20-25 years of standing in profession in
teaching and training and there should be same Selection Committee for all boards. Further the
Constitution of Members of National Councils and total number was not clear. There was also a
question mark on the Constitution of Ethics Committee. With regard to Clause No. 30, he was of the
view that the National Board of Health Education must also  be made responsible for exit examinations.
With regard to Clause No. 41, he was of the view that the Commission should not exempt any person,
enrolled as health practitioner in a country outside India as this may dilute the system. With regard
to Clause 35 (2) he was of the view that 10 years experience fo r member of National Evaluation and
Assessment Committee is inadequate as the same is equal to that of Additional Professor. With regard
to Clause 46, he was of the view that a provision must be made to include a member from each Union
Territory in the Council. Lastly with regard to Clause 47, the procedure and manner of Elections to
National Councils must be clearly prescribed.
